Enacted: December 14, 1960 | In force: 1961 | Amended: extensively
Establishes the compulsory social security system. Organizes the National Social Security Fund (CNSS for private sector) and the National Retirement and Social Welfare Fund (CNRPS for public sector). Covers retirement pensions, invalidity, survivors' benefits, occupational accidents, and maternity allowances.
LaborSocial

Occupational Accidents and Diseases Law — Law No. 94-28 of February 21, 1994

Enacted: February 21, 1994 | In force: 1994
Defines occupational accidents and diseases, establishes mandatory employer liability, and sets compensation levels for temporary or permanent disability and death. Requires employers to report occupational accidents within 2 days. Organizes compulsory insurance through CNSS.
Labor

Law No. 2025-9 of May 21, 2025Employment Reform Law

Enacted: May 21, 2025 | Published: JORT May 23, 2025
Landmark reform overhauling Tunisia's employment framework. Three pillars: (1) Indefinite employment contracts (CDI) are the statutory default; CDDs (fixed-term contracts) permitted only in three circumstances: temporary workload increase, replacement of absent worker, and seasonal work; any non-compliant CDD is void and automatically converted to CDI with full seniority. (2) Prohibition of labor subcontracting — workers supplied via subcontracting arrangements are deemed permanent employees of the beneficiary company; employers have 3 months to regularize. (3) Criminal penalty of TND 10,000 fine for engaging prohibited subcontracting. Contested by employer federations; supported by UGTT.
Labor

Law No. 2025-18 of December 22, 2025Public-Sector Recruitment of Long-Term Unemployed Graduates

Adopted by ARP: December 16, 2025 (92 for, 4 abstentions, 1 against) | Published: JORT No. 2025-153, December 23, 2025 | In force: December 23, 2025
An 8-article member's bill (sponsored by the "Pour que le peuple triomphe" parliamentary bloc) creating an exceptional mechanism for public-sector and civil-service recruitment of higher-education graduates in long-term unemployment, applied alongside the General Statute of Civil Servants above. Priority is given to candidates over 40 and to those whose diploma is 10+ years old, limited to one member per family, via a dedicated digital application platform. Excludes candidates who previously benefited from a regularization program, who had continuous social-security affiliation, who held a tax ID in the prior year, or who hold loans exceeding TND 40,000. Recruitment is staged in waves over up to 3 years; Finance Law 2026 (Law No. 2025-17) Art. 57 allocates budget for a first tranche of roughly 3,000 graduates. Status as of July 2026: implementing decrees remain unpublished; affected graduates staged protests (Siliana, reported July 1, 2026; a further protest planned for Tunis on July 8, 2026) demanding their publication. Update (Run 73): protest organizers put the affected pool at roughly 60,000 over-40 graduates out of some 137,000–140,000 unemployed higher-education graduates nationally, and specify three demands for the July 8 protest — publication of the implementing decrees, the promised digital application platform's launch, and a public implementation timetable (webdo.tn, directinfo.webmanagercenter.com, July 1, 2026). Update (Run 167): the July 8, 2026 protest occurred as planned — dozens of graduates demonstrated near Place de la Kasbah in Tunis renewing the same three demands — with the implementing decrees and digital platform still unpublished as of that date (webdo.tn, July 8, 2026). Update (Run 201): escalating beyond street protest, 52 ARP deputies sent a formal petition to the Head of Government on July 9, 2026 — over seven months after JORT publication — demanding the same three items: publication of the implementing decrees, launch of the digital application platform, and a public recruitment timetable (La Presse, July 9, 2026).
LaborAdministrative

Decrees No. 2026-63 through 2026-69 of April 30, 2026Public & Private Sector Wage Increase Package 2026–2028

Published: JORT No. 44, April 30, 2026 | Implements: Finance Law 2026 (Law No. 2025-17), Art. 15 | Effective: retroactive to January 1, 2026 | Covers: 2026–2028
A seven-decree package implementing Finance Law 2026 Art. 15 and Labor Code Art. 40, staging wage and pension increases across the entire public and private sectors over three years (2026–2028). Update (Run 38): previously this entry documented only the SMIG/SMAG/collective-agreement decrees (66–68); the package's other four decrees, covering the civil service, public enterprises, and the judiciary, are now added. Decree No. 2026-63 sets the raise program for agents of the state, local authorities, and administrative public establishments (fonctionnaires). Decree No. 2026-64 covers employees of public enterprises and establishments governed by special statutes or establishment-level collective agreements, staging monthly increases of TND 120 (cadres), TND 100–105 (supervisory/maîtrise), and TND 90 (execution-grade) across 2026–2028. Decree No. 2026-65 raises the "prime de magistrature" by TND 120/year (2026–2028) for judges of the judicial order, the Administrative Court, and the Court of Accounts, also extended to their retirement pensions. Decree No. 2026-66 sets the guaranteed agricultural minimum wage (SMAG) for workers 18 and over at TND 21.336/day in 2026, rising to TND 22.400 (2027) and TND 23.520 (2028), plus a technical-skill premium for specialized agricultural workers. Decree No. 2026-67 sets the guaranteed inter-professional minimum wage (SMIG) for non-agricultural sectors at TND 554.736/month (48-hour regime) or TND 470.251/month (40-hour regime) in 2026, rising in stages to TND 611.520 / TND 517.571 by 2028. Decree No. 2026-68 mandates an annual 5% increase in base salaries plus transport and attendance allowances for non-agricultural sectors covered by sectoral collective bargaining agreements, with monthly increases of roughly TND 36–46 for line workers, ~TND 53 for supervisory staff, and ~TND 69 for management-grade employees in 2026. Decree No. 2026-69 sets smaller, separately staged increases (roughly TND 30–39/month execution-grade, TND 44 maîtrise, TND 58–64 cadres in 2026, rising through 2028) for non-agricultural Labor Code employees covered by neither an enterprise-specific statute nor a sectoral collective agreement. A further schedule of monthly increases (TND 90–120, staged by category A1/A2/A3/B and phased through 2027–2028) applies to specified "sensitive" public-service corps, including health professionals (physicians, dentists, pharmacists, veterinarians), oversight/control bodies, scientific researchers, university academic staff, social-service personnel, and labor inspectors. Across all seven decrees, the increases extend to retirement pensions, are non-cumulative with certain pre-existing allowances, are excluded from year-end/performance-bonus calculations, and remain subject to standard social-security contributions; non-compliance with the SMIG/SMAG minimums is subject to the penalties under Art. 3 of the Labor Code Law of April 30, 1966.
LaborAdministrativeJudicial

Circular No. 11 of the Head of Government, August 11, 2025End of Trade-Union Secondment

Issued: August 11, 2025 (effective same day) | Reported: August 14, 2025
A circular from the Head of Government (then Sarra Zaafrani Zenzri) to all ministers and secretaries of state cancelling, effective August 11, 2025, all standing authorizations for "détachement syndical" — the practice of seconding civil servants to work full-time for trade-union structures while remaining on the public payroll. Ministries were instructed to compile lists of seconded agents, recall them to their original posts, and pursue "appropriate administrative and judicial procedures" against those who refuse. UGTT deputy secretary-general Sami Tahri called the measure "a political gesture" — arguing union secondment had "practically disappeared since 2022" — intended to "incite a war against union rights" rather than address a live administrative problem; some press subsequently referred to the instrument as "Decree No. 20," though the underlying text is a head-of-government circular, not a numbered decree. Confirmed via two independent French-language reports (webdo.tn and WebManagerCenter, both dated August 14, 2025) that agree on the August 11 effective date. Contemporaneous with, but legally distinct from, the Finance Law 2026 Art. 15 wage-setting-by-decree mechanism above, which separately bypassed UGTT's traditional role in tripartite wage negotiation — together read by UGTT and independent commentators as a broader narrowing of the union's institutional role in 2025–2026.
Labor2025

Law No. 2024-44 of August 12, 2024Maternity and Paternity Leave Reform

Adopted by ARP: July 31, 2024 (111-1-4) | Published: JORT No. 2024-099, August 12, 2024
Restructures maternity and paternity leave across the public and private sectors. Expands paternity leave from the prior baseline of 1 day (private sector, Labor Code) / 2 days (civil servants) to 7 days at full pay — extended to 10 days for multiple births or a newborn with disability, prematurity, or a congenital condition requiring care, plus 3 additional days in the event of stillbirth; must be taken within 30 days of the birth. Restructures maternity leave into a prenatal component (up to 15 days in the final month, on medical order) and a postnatal component extended from 30 to 90 days (120 days for complicated or multiple births).
LaborSocialPersonal Status

Law No. 93-10 of February 17, 1993 (as amended)Vocational Training Law

Enacted: February 17, 1993 | In force: 1993 | Amended: multiple times
Framework law governing vocational and professional training in Tunisia. Creates the National Agency for Vocational Training (ATFP — Agence Tunisienne de la Formation Professionnelle). Establishes the national apprenticeship system, certification standards, and quality assurance for vocational qualifications. Employers must contribute 1% of payroll to the Vocational Training Fund (TFP).
LaborEducation

Digital Platform ("Gig-Economy") Workers — Regulatory Vacuum (Run 99 addition)

UGTT study launched: June 2025 | Findings presented: June 29, 2026
A genuine legislative gap, previously undocumented despite 98 prior research runs: Tunisia has no law, decree, or Labor Code amendment specifically classifying or regulating digital-platform ("gig-economy") workers — the general Labor Code (No. 66-27, above) applies loosely at best, since most platform workers are treated as independent contractors rather than employees. A UGTT research-department study, launched June 2025 and presented June 29, 2026, found roughly 60,000 workers across four categories (commercial order delivery, pharmacy/medicine delivery, home care/caregiving, and motorcycle passenger transport) operating in what it called the "parallel economy," entirely outside any legal framework: 98% lack any social coverage, 60% of delivery workers are denied all insurance, and 75% suffer irregular income. UGTT's recommendations: integrate platform workers into the CNSS social-security regime, mandate clear written contracts defining the parties' relationship, create professional/union representation mechanisms, provide occupational health and safety training, and establish a national database of platform workers. The union also invoked newly-adopted ILO Convention No. 193 on decent work in the platform economy (see the International Law & Treaties section's companion entry) as a reference framework. No Tunisian bill or draft text addressing platform work has been filed with the ARP as of this run; corroborated across Business News, L'Économiste Maghrébin, WebManagerCenter, La Presse de Tunisie, Gnet News, and allAfrica, all dated June 29–30, 2026. Update (Run 378): the government moved from documenting the gap to publicly proposing to close it: Minister of Social Affairs Issam Lahmar, opening a July 14, 2026 workshop on new forms of employment and decent work, called for adapting several Labor Code provisions to establish a balanced framework covering digital-platform work, and for deepened tripartite consultation toward an inclusive national approach (La Presse de Tunisie, July 14, 2026). UGTT responded on July 17, 2026 by demanding formal, mandatory inclusion of its social-protection and informal-sector department in the technical committees that will draft the resulting sectoral bills, specifically naming delivery workers' (livreurs) and ride-hailing drivers' (chauffeurs) legal status as the priority to address within the Labor Code revision (La Presse de Tunisie and allAfrica, both July 17, 2026). Still no bill text has been filed with the ARP on gig/delivery-platform work specifically — see the separate, already-filed freelance/digital-economy self-employment bill below, which addresses a related but distinct category of independent digital workers. Update (Run 438): Minister Lahmar advanced from the July 14 workshop's general call to a concrete two-bill framing: opening the 8th Arab Conference on Pensions and Social Insurance in Tunis (July 20–22, 2026), he announced that two draft laws are nearing finalization as part of a broader social-security/protection-system overhaul — one dedicated to regulating private-sector telework, the other specifically to the digital "platform economy," intended to give new forms of employment a legal framework, support the underlying economic activity, and extend social protection to the workers concerned (Business News and La Presse de Tunisie, both July 20, 2026; corroborated by allAfrica same-day). Still pre-filing — neither bill had been transmitted to the ARP as of this update.
LaborSocialGap

Freelance Work & Digital-Economy Self-Employment Bill (Proposition de loi)

Filed at the ARP: June 9, 2026 | Status: pending committee examination, not yet enacted
A member's bill (proposition de loi) filed with the Assembly of the Representatives of the People on June 9, 2026, creating for the first time a dedicated legal framework for independent ("freelance") workers operating in Tunisia's digital economy, distinct from the gig/delivery-platform gap tracked in the companion entry above. Structured in six substantive chapters plus a seventh on transitional provisions. Chapter I sets general provisions and defines the bill's core concepts: independent/freelance work, the legal status of the freelance worker, electronic-payment platforms, and dedicated bank accounts for freelance-related transactions. A further chapter sets out state obligations to support independent workers, including facilitating access to the global digital-services market and to electronic payment for cross-border freelance income (Business News, June 16, 2026). The transitional-provisions chapter establishes a phase-in period of not less than five years, during which caps on certain electronic-payment and funds-transfer operations, together with security and financial-verification (anti-money-laundering/KYC-style) procedures, apply on a graduated basis to manage the sector's controlled integration into the formal economy — addressing long-standing complaints from Tunisian freelancers about restricted access to international payment platforms (PayPal, Payoneer, and similar services) and the foreign-currency repatriation rules under the Foreign Exchange Code (see that entry). As of this update, the bill remains at the pre-committee stage at the ARP; no committee referral, hearing schedule, or vote has been reported (Business News, June 16, 2026; Directinfo/WebManagerCenter, June 16, 2026).
LaborCommercial2026Pending

🏛️ Administrative Law

Administrative Court Law — Law No. 72-40 of June 1, 1972

Enacted: June 1, 1972 | In force: 1972 | Amended: multiple times
Establishes the Administrative Court (Tribunal Administratif) as the supreme administrative jurisdiction. Has jurisdiction over disputes between citizens and public bodies, annulment of illegal administrative acts, and administrative contracts. Also serves as the Supreme Audit Institution reviewing public accounts. Organized into chambers; appeals go to Administrative Court plenary.
Administrative

Local Government Code — Law No. 2018-29 of May 9, 2018Majallat Al-Jama'aat Al-Mahaliyya

Enacted: May 9, 2018 | In force: May 2018 | Implements: 2014 Constitution Chapter VII
Comprehensive decentralization legislation replacing the previous municipalities law. Organizes three levels of local government: municipalities (balladiyyat), regions (wilayat), and districts (aqalim). Establishes directly-elected municipal councils with legislative powers in local matters. Introduces participatory budgeting and citizens' right to petition. Implemented after 2018 municipal elections. Superseded in part: Organic Law No. 2025-4 of March 12, 2025 (JORT No. 30, March 13, 2025; adopted by the ARP February 27, 2025, 109–2–4) repealed this code's region and district provisions (and Organic Law No. 89-11 of February 4, 1989 on regional councils), replacing the elected-region model with local, regional, and district councils organized by decree, and transferring former regional councils' assets and holdings to the State for governors to administer. The 2018 code's municipal-level provisions remain in force; local council elections continue to be organized under Decree-Law No. 2023-10 of March 8, 2023 (see the CNRD entry in Constitutional Law).
Key provisions
  • Three levels: municipality, region (governorate), district
  • Directly elected councils at all three levels
  • Fiscal autonomy — own revenue sources and transfers from central budget
  • Participatory democracy mechanisms (citizen petitions, public consultation)
  • Gender parity requirement for municipal council lists
  • Youth quota (minimum 1/3 of each list under 35)
Administrative

Public Procurement Decree — Decree No. 2014-1039 of March 13, 2014

Enacted: March 13, 2014 | In force: 2014 | Amended: 2022
Governs all public procurement by state entities, public establishments, and local authorities. Establishes competitive bidding requirements, transparency obligations, evaluation criteria, and remedies for tenderers. Creates the National Commission for Public Procurement Investigations (HAICOP). Sets thresholds for direct award, restricted tendering, and open tendering. Pending reform (added Run 122): Head of Government Sarra Zaâfrani Zenzri presided over a Council of Ministers session on November 22, 2025 devoted to overhauling the public-procurement system, described in a January 3, 2026 report as "one of the most important legislative reforms currently under examination" for its expected effect on investment and competition; as of this run the reform remains at the policy-objective stage, with no decree or bill number yet filed to amend or replace Decree No. 2014-1039.
Administrative

Access to Information Decree — Decree-Law No. 2011-41 of May 26, 2011

Enacted: May 26, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Strengthened by 2016 Organic Law
Grants citizens the right to access information held by public bodies. Establishes a 20-day response deadline for requests. Replaced in part by the more comprehensive Organic Law No. 2016-22 of March 24, 2016, which created an independent Access to Information Commission (IVD predecessor).
AdministrativePress

Law No. 2016-46 of July 11, 2016Electronic Procedures in Administrative Transactions

Enacted: July 11, 2016 | In force: 2017
Authorizes and regulates e-government services. Allows administrative procedures to be conducted electronically. Establishes standards for electronic authentication, digital archives, and online payment to the state. Part of Tunisia's e-government reform strategy. (Run 111) A "zero paper" administrative-digitalization measure took effect July 1, 2026, extending electronic authentication and paperless processing across further categories of state administrative transactions under this law's framework.
AdministrativeDigital

Law No. 2021-35 of July 21, 2021Administrative Procedure Law

Enacted: July 21, 2021 | In force: 2021 | Partially implemented
Codifies and standardizes administrative procedures, notification requirements, and citizens' rights when dealing with public bodies. Establishes the "silence means approval" principle for certain administrative decisions (within 30 days). Strengthens citizens' right to be heard before adverse administrative decisions. Partially implemented pending regulatory decrees.
Administrative

Law No. 2017-58 addendum / Circular No. 39 of 2017Civil Service Reform

Enacted: 2017
Introduces performance management and results-based evaluation for civil servants. Creates management by objectives (MBO) framework for public entities. Reorganizes career tracks and introduces merit-based promotion criteria. Part of broader public administration reform supported by World Bank.
AdministrativeLabor

Law No. 99-32 of April 13, 1999National Statistics System (Run 81 addition)

Enacted: April 13, 1999 | Published: JORT | In force: 1999
A wholly new standalone entry — despite 80 prior research runs, no dedicated entry existed for the statute organizing Tunisia's official statistics. Confirmed via direct reading of the law's full 29-article primary text (hosted by the National Institute of Statistics, ins.tn) cross-checked against legislation-securite.tn. Establishes the "Système National de la Statistique" (Art. 12), comprising the National Statistics Council (CNS), the National Institute of Statistics (INS) as the system's central executive body (Art. 18–19), other public statistical bodies, and statistical-training institutions. Article 3 guarantees the system's structures scientific/methodological independence and impartiality in their statistical work, and Article 5 imposes a 60-year confidentiality rule on individual survey data collected under the law.
Administrative

Law No. 82-85 of December 2, 1982Engineers Profession Organization

Enacted: December 2, 1982, ratifying Decree-Law No. 82-12 of October 21, 1982 | Amended: Law No. 97-41 of June 9, 1997
Creates the Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens (OIT) and restricts legal use of the professional title "ingénieur" — and provision of engineering services under that title — to persons registered on the Order's roll; an engineering diploma alone does not confer the right to use the title. OIT's missions include upholding professional honor and ethics and advising on national engineering-workforce planning. A reform petition to modernize OIT governance was reported circulating among engineers as of late 2025, with no bill filed with the ARP as of this run. (Run 90 addition)
Administrative

Administrative Mediator — Law No. 93-51 of May 3, 1993Médiateur Administratif (Run 179 addition)

Institution created: Decree No. 92-2143 of December 10, 1992 | Legal framework: Law No. 93-51 of May 3, 1993 | Amended: Law No. 2000-16 of February 7, 2000; Law No. 2002-21 of February 14, 2002 | Attributions/organization decree: Decree No. 96-1126 of June 15, 1996
A wholly new standalone entry — despite 178 prior research runs across this section, no dedicated entry existed for Tunisia's ombudsman-equivalent institution. The Médiateur Administratif ("Administrative Mediator") was created by Decree No. 92-2143 of December 10, 1992 and given its operating legal framework by Law No. 93-51 of May 3, 1993, later extended by Law No. 2000-16 of February 7, 2000 and Law No. 2002-21 of February 14, 2002; Decree No. 96-1126 of June 15, 1996 (superseding the 1992 decree's own provisions) fixes the institution's attributions and its administrative/financial organization. Appointed by decree for a renewable five-year term, the Mediator examines individual and corporate complaints against state services, local authorities, public establishments, and public enterprises, and — per the law's own independence guarantee — "receives no instructions from any public authority" while examining a request. The Mediator directs the institution and administers its own budget, which attaches to the general state budget but falls under the Presidency's direct authority; regional mediation offices were added following the 2000 amendment to extend the service nationwide. Recommendations issued are non-binding, distinct from the judicially enforceable rulings of the Administrative Court (Law No. 72-40, above). Tunisia's Administrative Mediator is a member of the Association des Ombudsmans et Médiateurs de la Francophonie (AOMF). Confirmed via legislation-securite.tn's primary-text reproductions of Law No. 93-51 and Decree No. 96-1126, and the AOMF's institutional profile of Tunisia.
Administrative

Proposition de Loi Organique (unnumbered, ~10 deputies)Administrative Transparency & Anti-Lobbying Bill (Run 284 addition)

Reported: July 10, 2026 | Status: recently submitted to ARP Commission de la Législation Générale | Not yet numbered | Not yet enacted
A deputy-initiated organic bill of 19 articles, carried by a group reported as around ten deputies, aimed at curbing "powerful lobbies" and administrative non-compliance/evasion by public officials. Per a July 10, 2026 La Presse editorial ("Quand la justice retrouve ses clés") and a July 11, 2026 allAfrica republication, the text introduces prison sentences of up to three years and increased, "finally dissuasive" fines, and imposes a transparency obligation on administrations via mandatory annual reports, framed by the source as a first step toward institutional accountability. Caveat: neither article named the bill's official title, an assigned proposition/projet de loi number, or the sponsoring deputies, and a direct fetch of the La Presse source (its primary framing is a broader opinion piece on judicial enforcement) likewise did not surface these details — this entry is added on the strength of the specific, dated (July 10–11, 2026), multi-outlet-corroborated factual claims (19 articles, ~10 deputies, 3-year maximum prison term, annual-report transparency mechanism) while the bill's formal identifiers remain unconfirmed and are a priority to pin down in a subsequent run. Distinct from the unrelated, already-documented Public Procurement reform (still at the policy-objective stage, no bill filed) and the Tax Adviser organic bill (proposition n° 2023-013) above.
AdministrativePending

💰 Tax & Fiscal Law

Income Tax Code — Law No. 89-114 of December 30, 1989Code de l'Impôt sur le Revenu des Personnes Physiques et l'Impôt sur les Sociétés

Enacted: December 30, 1989 | In force: January 1, 1990 | Amended: annually in Finance Laws
Governs personal income tax (IRPP) and corporate income tax (IS) in Tunisia. Personal income tax is progressive with rates from 0% to 35%. Corporate income tax standard rate is 15%, with higher rates for banks and telecoms (35%) and reduced rates for SMEs and certain sectors. Amended annually through the Finance Law. Digital fiscal cash-register mandate (added Run 280): a Ministerial Order of October 14, 2025, implementing CIRPP/IS Art. 59 ter, phases in mandatory digital cash registers ("caisses enregistreuses numériques") for on-site food/beverage-consumption businesses (restaurants, cafés, tea rooms, fast food), defined as any establishment selling prepared food or drink for consumption on the premises. Phase 1 (November 1, 2025): classified tourist restaurants and 2nd/3rd-category cafés. Phase 2 (July 1, 2026): extended to all other legal entities (companies) in the sector. Phase 3 (July 1, 2027): extends to real-regime individual taxpayers filing monthly returns. Phase 4 (July 1, 2028): extends to remaining individual operators. Distinct from the Finance Law 2026 Art. 56 e-invoicing mandate (see below), which governs invoice issuance rather than point-of-sale transaction recording; confirmed via La Presse, Tunisie Numérique, Le Temps News, and L'Economiste Maghrébin, cross-checked against each other.
Key rates (as of 2024)
  • Personal income tax: 0% up to 5,000 TND, 26% bracket, 35% top rate
  • Corporate income tax: 15% standard rate
  • Banks, financial institutions, telecoms: 35%
  • SMEs: reduced rate of 10% (conditions apply)
  • Withholding tax on dividends: 10%
  • Capital gains tax: included in income tax
TaxAmended

VAT Code — Law No. 88-61 of June 2, 1988Code de la Taxe sur la Valeur Ajoutée

Enacted: June 2, 1988 | In force: January 1, 1989 | Amended: annually
Establishes value-added tax in Tunisia. Standard rate 19%; reduced rates of 13% (certain services) and 7% (basic goods, agricultural products). Exports are zero-rated. Registration required for businesses with turnover exceeding 100,000 TND/year (services) or 200,000 TND (goods).
TaxAmended

Tax Procedures Code — Law No. 2000-82 of August 9, 2000Code des Droits et Procédures Fiscaux

Enacted: August 9, 2000 | In force: 2001 | Amended: annually
Unifies and governs all tax administration procedures including filing obligations, tax audits, assessment, payment, penalties, and dispute resolution. Establishes taxpayers' rights during tax audits. Provides for administrative appeals before tax committees and judicial appeals before administrative courts.
Tax

Customs Code — Law No. 2008-77 of December 22, 2008

Enacted: December 22, 2008 | In force: 2009 | Replaced: 1955 Customs Code
Governs importation, exportation, transit, and customs duties. Establishes the legal framework for customs declarations, inspections, duty rates, and customs offenses. Aligns Tunisia's customs procedures with international standards (WCO Revised Kyoto Convention). Governs free zones and customs warehouses.
TaxCommercial

Local Taxes Law — Law No. 97-11 of February 3, 1997

Enacted: February 3, 1997 | In force: 1997 | Amended: multiple times
Regulates taxes and fees collected by municipalities including property taxes (TFP — taxe foncière sur les propriétés bâties et non bâties), tourism taxes, advertising taxes, and local business taxes (TCL — taxe sur les établissements à caractère industriel, commercial ou professionnel).
TaxAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-79 of December 22, 2022Finance Law 2023

Enacted: December 22, 2022 | Published: JORT No. 141, December 23, 2022 | In force: January 1, 2023
Annual budget law, exceptionally issued as a presidential decree-law rather than an ordinary parliamentary statute because the Assembly of People's Representatives had not yet resumed full legislative activity following the December 2022 legislative elections. Set state expenditure at approximately TND 54 billion against TND 46.4 billion in revenue (deficit of approximately TND 7.5 billion). Introduced a real-estate wealth tax on individuals' property holdings worth TND 3 million or more, and temporary customs/VAT relief on EV charging equipment.
Tax

Law No. 2023-13 of December 11, 2023Finance Law 2024

Adopted by ARP: December 10, 2023 (116 for, 4 against, 26 abstentions) | Enacted: December 11, 2023 | Published: JORT, December 12, 2023 | Fiscal year: 2024
First annual Finance Law passed by the elected Assembly of People's Representatives since 2021. Key changes: Corporate Income Tax (CIT) increased from 15% to 20% for general companies effective January 1, 2024. Bank and insurance company CIT rate increased from 35% to 40%. New 2% conjunctural contribution on FY2023 taxable income for companies with turnover ≥ TND 20 million. Standard deduction for rental income raised from 20% to 25%. New 4-year CIT/IRPP exemption for enterprises filing investment declarations in 2024–2025. Raised import duties on solar panels from 10% to 30% effective January 1, 2025.
TaxAmended

Law No. 2024-48 of December 9, 2024Finance Law 2025

Adopted by ARP/CNRD: December 2 & 4, 2024 | Enacted: December 9, 2024 | Published: JORT No. 149, December 10, 2024 | Fiscal year: 2025
Key measures: VAT reduced to 7% (from 13%) on low-voltage electricity for domestic consumers using ≤300 kWh/month. New conjunctural budget contribution (2% on FY2024 taxable income) for large companies (turnover ≥ TND 20M). Additional tax incentives for R&D-intensive companies. Community enterprises exempt from VAT under new suspension regime. Reinforces tax inspection powers for real estate valuations. Raises low pensions to TND 260 with roughly 7–7.5% increases for retirees, plus income-tax relief for pensioners, and begins phasing in mandatory electronic invoicing for an expanded range of service providers.
TaxAmended

Law No. 2025-17 of December 12, 2025Finance Law 2026

Adopted: ARP December 4, 2025 (89 for/23 against/12 abstain); Council of Regions and Districts December 7, 2025 | Published: JORT No. 148, December 12, 2025 | Fiscal year: 2026
State budget sets revenues at TND 52.56 billion and expenditures at TND 63.575 billion, with tax receipts of TND 47.773 billion as the dominant funding source. Key measures: Art. 88 establishes a new annual "wealth tax" (impôt sur la fortune) on Tunisian-resident individuals' worldwide net patrimony (non-residents taxed only on Tunisian-situs assets, subject to double-tax treaties), at 0.5% on net wealth between TND 3–5 million and 1% above TND 5 million, assessed as of January 1 each year including minor children's assets held in trust; exempts the taxpayer's principal residence, professional assets recorded on a business balance sheet, home-savings and investment-savings accounts, life insurance/capitalization contracts, and non-utility vehicles rated ≤12 fiscal horsepower; declared electronically by end of June each year, first applying to the 2026 tax year; implementing guidance issued via Note Commune n°13 (DGELF, June 11, 2026) and n°15. Art. 56 expands mandatory e-invoicing from goods transactions to the entire services sector (~380,000 professionals affected), effective January 1, 2026 — invoices must be issued through the "El Fatoora" platform operated by Tunisie TradeNet (TTN) in the standardized TEIF/XML format and bear a qualified electronic signature issued by TunTrust/ANCE; penalties run 100–500 TND per invoice (capped at 50,000 TND/year) for invoices wrongly issued outside the electronic system where e-invoicing is mandatory, and 250–10,000 TND (doubled for repeat offenses) for non-compliant electronic invoices; tax incentives for new/used vehicle imports for resident families; state-subsidized interest bonuses (up to 3 points) on investment credits taken out in 2026–2027; salary and pension increases staged across 2026–2028 by decree; VAT exemption on packaging inputs for locally produced olive oil; VAT reduced to 7% on EV charging equipment and lithium battery manufacturing inputs; Art. 135 (added Run 130) authorizes farmers to import, possess, and use drones for agricultural purposes (field surveillance, irrigation optimization, phytosanitary treatment) — adopted as an additional article by ARP vote of 60–29–14 on December 4, 2025; see the Civil Aviation Code entry for the separate, still-pending general civil-drone regulatory framework. A parliamentary amendment adopted December 2, 2025 (69–17–17) additionally authorizes Tunisian residents to open and hold foreign-currency bank accounts for the first time — see Exchange Control Law entry. Tunisian financial-press sources (ilBoursa, InFirst Auditors) also report that the FY2024–2025 "conjunctural contribution" of 4% on taxable profit — previously limited to banks, financial establishments, and insurers (see the Finance Law 2024 entry) — was made permanent under this law and extended to telecommunications network operators and automobile dealers/concessionaires; this extension is corroborated by two independent secondary sources but the specific implementing article number could not be confirmed against a primary JORT text during this research pass, so it is noted here with that caveat. Art. 83 (added Run 109): revises tax and customs advantages for Tunisians residing abroad (TRE) — full customs-duty exemption on electric and plug-in-hybrid vehicle imports (VAT reduced to 7%, customs-service fees to 3%), a once-per-five-years equipment/materials import benefit (plus a separate single-truck allowance under tariff heading 8704) for TRE-backed investment, extension, or participation projects in Tunisia, and exemption from filing a Tunisian tax return for TRE who present a foreign tax-residency certificate — corroborated across Managers.tn, Tunisie Numérique, La Presse de Tunisie, and InFirst Auditors' commentary on the law; see also the National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad entry (Nationality section). Pending amendment (added Run 118): the law's core assumptions have been overtaken by an oil-price shock — the budget was set on a reference Brent price of USD 63.3/barrel, versus actual prices approaching USD 110/barrel by June 2026 (each additional dollar costing roughly TND 160 million; a financing gap estimated at TND 5–8 billion by year-end). ARP Finance and Budget Commission president Maher Ktari stated on radio June 9, 2026 that recourse to a "loi de finances rectificative" (or, per some economists' competing proposal, a more flexible scenario-based "loi de finances d'urgence") in September or October 2026 is now "inevitable," while criticizing the Finance Ministry for not yet engaging the commission on the amendment's substance; a ministerial meeting to examine the discrepancy was scheduled for July 2026. No amending bill text had been filed with the ARP as of this run — corroborated across La Presse/allAfrica, Business News, Kapitalis, Tustex, and webdo, but treat as a forecast/committee-pressure signal, not yet a pending bill with a registry number. Update (Run 544): a July 25, 2026 La Presse report narrows the timeline first flagged at Run 118 — Ktari (also transliterated "El Kattari") stated the reference oil price had by then been exceeded by over USD 100/barrel (rather than the roughly USD 110/barrel figure cited in June), that each additional dollar per barrel now adds an estimated TND 164 million to compensation-fund expenditure (versus the TND ~160 million figure previously on file), and that a "loi de finances complémentaire" for 2026 could specifically be submitted to the ARP in October 2026 — narrower than the earlier "September or October" framing — with an urgent year-end financing need estimated at TND 5 billion. Still no bill text filed with the ARP as of this update; the underlying forecast is unchanged in substance, only refined in figures and timing. Also effective January 1, 2026 (added Run 120): this law repealed Article 45 of the Finance Law 2019 (Law No. 2018-56 of December 27, 2018), which had capped cash payments at TND 5,000 — payments above that threshold are again permitted — and introduced a new TND 230 registration tax on donation contracts (donations) between spouses, ascendants, and descendants. Tax amnesty (added Run 127): Art. 69 establishes a 2026 tax-debt regularization mechanism, operationalized by Tax Administration Circular No. 4/2026 ("JIBAYA"): control penalties, late-payment penalties, and prosecution fees are waived for taxpayers who settle principal tax debt (in full or via an installment plan) with the tax authority by June 30, 2026, and separately for unfiled, omitted, or rectificative declarations and unregistered acts/contracts subject to registration duties (Fiscal Procedures Code Arts. 81, 82 & 85) relating to obligations due before October 31, 2025, if regularized by September 30, 2026. Art. 36 implementing decree (added Run 327): a joint order of the Ministers of Social Affairs, Health, and Finance, published in JORT on or around July 14, 2026, sets the conditions and procedure for the monthly TND 130 allowance under Art. 36 for people with xeroderma pigmentosum ("enfants de la lune") toward the cost of UV-protection equipment (sun-filtering products, protective clothing), and a parallel monthly allowance for gluten-intolerant/celiac patients from low-income families toward the cost of gluten-free food products; both require an application with supporting medical documentation, reviewed by a dedicated medical commission — corroborated by La Presse de Tunisie, Directinfo/WebManagerCenter, and allAfrica (all July 14–15, 2026); the exact JORT issue number was not independently confirmed across these sources and is therefore omitted rather than guessed. Large-holdings restructuring fund (added Run 407): the law also creates a dedicated 2026-budget financing line for restructuring large agricultural exploitations exceeding 1,000 hectares, funded from revenue generated by regularizing previously undeclared agricultural wells (a measure carried over from the Finance Law 2025); however, the article number is disputed between the two independent sources found — Tunisie Numérique cites Art. 138 and frames it as an Office des Terres Domaniales fund targeting state-owned ("domanial") parcels in Sfax, Siliana, Béja, and Jendouba, while CKF's professional tax commentary cites Art. 97 and frames it more generally as covering "exploitations agricoles internationales" without naming specific governorates or the Office des Terres Domaniales. Both sources agree on the >1,000-hectare threshold and the restructuring purpose; neither could be cross-checked against primary JORT text during this pass, so the article number and precise scope are flagged here as unresolved rather than asserted, pending a future run's access to the primary text.
TaxAmendedPending

Proposition de Loi Organique n° 2023-013Statute of the Tax Adviser Profession

Filed: 2023 | ARP Commission de la Législation Générale review ongoing: article-by-article vote October 23, 2025; disputed-article sessions June 3 and June 18, 2026 | Not yet finalized | Not yet enacted
A deputy-initiated organic bill (proposition de loi organique n° 2023-013, also cited as 13/2023) to create and regulate a licensed "conseiller fiscal" (tax adviser) profession in Tunisia, defining qualification requirements, a professional order, and the rights and duties of practitioners advising on tax matters. Reviewed by the Assembly of People's Representatives' Commission de la Législation Générale since at least mid-2025; the Ministries of Finance and Justice raised objections concerning overlap with the Code des Droits et Procédures Fiscaux and existing rules governing the legal (lawyer) profession. The commission held an article-by-article vote on the full bill text on October 23, 2025, but a residual set of "disputed articles" (fasl khilafiya) remained unresolved. Correction: examination was not finalized by early June as previously tracked — a June 3, 2026 session (following an earlier study day) specifically reviewed those remaining disputed articles, and the commission resumed again on June 18, 2026 to continue that same review and set its forward work program; no finalization or vote has been confirmed since. Status as of July 2026: still in committee review, not yet scheduled for plenary, not yet adopted.
TaxAdministrative

Draft Finance Law 2027 (PLF 2027)Budget-Preparation Social Dialogue

Finance Ministry invited UGTT contribution: reported May 15, 2026 | UGTT transmitted formal proposals: ~July 1–2, 2026 | Ministry's registry office declined to formally receive the document: reported July 2–4, 2026
Tunisia's 2027 budget law remains at the pre-draft, social-dialogue stage — no PLF 2027 bill text has been circulated publicly as of this pass, a genuine gap distinct from the still-pending Finance Law 2026 amendment discussed above. The Finance Ministry invited UGTT to contribute proposals for the 2027 budget in mid-May 2026; UGTT Secretary-General Slaheddine Selmi called the invitation a positive step but was explicitly skeptical it signaled a genuine return of social dialogue after months of rupture between the union and government (echoing his stance already documented in the Judicial Statute entry's ONAT-related coverage). UGTT's proposals, formally transmitted in early July 2026, include raising the fully tax-exempt income threshold from TND 5,000 to TND 7,000, revising the IRPP income-tax brackets to ease pressure on the upper-middle class, increasing public investment, rebalancing debt policy, and reviving budget-related social dialogue; the union separately criticized an "austerity orientation" it said risked public-service quality and cautioned against curbs on professional promotions. In early July 2026, the Ministry of Finance's own registry office declined to formally receive UGTT's proposal document, which Selmi characterized as a setback for recognition of the union's role as a social partner. No PLF 2027 draft text, committee referral, or vote had occurred as of this pass. (Added Run 481.)
TaxFinancialPending

🏦 Financial & Banking Law

Banking Law — Law No. 2016-48 of July 11, 2016

Enacted: July 11, 2016 | In force: 2016 | Replaced: Law No. 2001-65
Regulates credit institutions, banking activities, and financial services in Tunisia. Establishes licensing requirements for banks and microfinance institutions. Strengthens the supervisory powers of the Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT). Introduces new categories of banking licenses and regulates Islamic banking activities.
Key provisions
  • Licensing: exclusive reserve of banking activities for licensed credit institutions
  • Capital requirements: minimum TND 25 million for universal banks
  • Supervision: BCT has powers to inspect, sanction, and revoke licenses
  • Islamic banking: participatory banks regulated under specific provisions
  • Deposit guarantee: Art. 149 creates the Fonds de Garantie des Dépôts Bancaires (FGDB)
  • Resolution: framework for failing banks established
Financial

Central Bank Law — Law No. 2016-35 of April 25, 2016

Enacted: April 25, 2016 | In force: 2016 | Replaced: Law No. 58-90 (1958) | Amended: 2024, 2026
Establishes the Banque Centrale de Tunisie (BCT) as an independent institution responsible for monetary policy, exchange rate management, payment systems oversight, and banking supervision. Grants operational independence to the BCT Governor. Article 25 originally prohibited direct central bank financing of the state budget deficit. This prohibition was exceptionally suspended by Law No. 2024-10 of February 7, 2024 (JORT No. 2024-021, adopted by the ARP 92 votes to 41), authorizing the BCT to lend the Treasury up to TND 7,000 million, interest-free, repayable over 10 years with a 3-year grace period, to help cover the 2024 budget deficit — a measure Carnegie Endowment and TIMEP analyses describe as eroding central bank independence. Update (Run 40): Finance Law 2026 Art. 12 authorizes a further, separate interest-free BCT advance to the Treasury of up to TND 11,000 million (up from TND 7,000 million in 2024/2025). Independent of fiscal financing, the BCT cut its policy rate in three steps from 8% to 7% between late 2025 and March 2026 (held at 7% at its March 30, 2026 meeting); a March 26, 2026 circular (No. 2026-4) newly restricts bank financing of "non-priority" imports, generally requiring 100% importer-funded deposits; and in January 2026 the BCT granted OFT Tunisie (Ooredoo's fintech arm) a definitive payment-institution license (JORT No. 65/2026). Update (Run 191): on July 7–8, 2026 the BCT signed an expanded balance-of-payments support facility with the Arab Monetary Fund worth US$312 million (76.7 billion Arab accounting dinars), disbursed in three tranches over a seven-year maturity (La Presse de Tunisie, Tustex).
FinancialAmended

Insurance Code — Law No. 92-24 of March 9, 1992

Enacted: March 9, 1992 (JORT No. 17 of March 17, 1992) | In force: 1992 | Amended: 1997, 2002, 2008, 2014
Governs all insurance and reinsurance activities in Tunisia. Mandatory insurance categories include third-party motor liability, employer liability, and professional liability for certain professions. Regulates insurance contract terms, claims procedures, and policyholder protections. Insurance companies supervised by the Insurance General Committee (CGA). Pending reform: on March 29, 2026, 14 ARP deputies filed a bill to raise road-accident compensation caps under the motor-liability provisions introduced by amending Law No. 2005-86: proposed indexation to 5× annual income for a surviving spouse, 10× the SMIG for victims without stable income, a 3-year SMIG supplement for breadwinners, and triennial inflation-linked revision of caps. Not yet enacted as of June 2026; the exact proposition number was not independently confirmed. (Added Run 139) Distinct from that pending cap-raising bill, the underlying Fonds de Garantie des Victimes des Accidents de la Circulation (Traffic Accident Victims Guarantee Fund) — created within this Code (Title V, Ch. III, Arts. 172–176, added by Law No. 2005-86 of August 15, 2005) to compensate accident victims or heirs when the at-fault driver is unknown, uninsured, or coverage is void/suspended — was itself restructured by Article 18 of Finance Law 2025 (Law No. 2024-48): effective January 1, 2025, Insurance Code Arts. 172 and 174–176 were repealed and the fund was converted into a special Treasury account administered by the ministry overseeing state domains, funded by a 0.2% levy on insurers' net motor-liability premiums plus a 2% levy on insureds' premiums (collected monthly alongside the unified insurance tax), with continuing subrogation rights against at-fault parties. A wholly new, previously undocumented enacted-law detail — not a status change to the pending cap-raising bill above.
Financial

Microfinance Law — Law No. 2011-117 of November 5, 2011

Enacted: November 5, 2011 | In force: 2011
Liberalizes and regulates the microfinance sector, allowing private and associative organizations to offer microcredit services beyond the former state monopoly (Banque Tunisienne de Solidarité). Establishes the Supervisory Authority for Microfinance (ACM). Maximum loan amounts and interest rate caps specified.
FinancialSocial

Financial Market Law — Law No. 94-117 of November 14, 1994

Enacted: November 14, 1994 | In force: 1995 | Amended: 1999 (Law 99-92), 2005 (Law 2005-96), 2009, 2019 (Law 2019-47)
Governs the Tunisian stock exchange (Bourse des Valeurs Mobilières de Tunis — BVMT), securities regulation, and capital markets. Creates the Financial Market Council (CMF — Conseil du Marché Financier) as the securities regulator. Regulates public offers, disclosure requirements, insider trading, and market manipulation. (Run 145: amendment-year list corrected — a previously listed "2016" amendment could not be independently verified and was replaced with confirmed 1999/2005/2009/2019 amendments.)
FinancialCommercial

Deposit Guarantee Fund — Art. 149 of Law No. 2016-48 of July 11, 2016Fonds de Garantie des Dépôts Bancaires (FGDB) (Run 172 correction)

Founding law: July 11, 2016 (Art. 149) | Implementing decree: No. 2017-268 of February 1, 2017 | Replaced: Law No. 2006-19 of May 2, 2006
Correction: this entry previously cited the fund's 2006 predecessor (Law No. 2006-19, "FGTD," TND 20,000 cap) as still governing; independent verification against the fund's own official site (fgdb.gov.tn) confirms the deposit-guarantee scheme was re-created under Article 149 of the 2016 Banking Law, not merely amended, as the Fonds de Garantie des Dépôts Bancaires (FGDB) — a public-interest establishment with legal personality, capitalized at TND 5 million and jointly subscribed by the Tunisian State and the Central Bank (BCT). Compensation is automatic (no depositor application required), capped at TND 60,000 per depositor per bank across all accounts, payable within 20 working days of a payout decision; member banks pay an annual contribution of 0.3% of their outstanding deposits. Administered under BCT supervision; covers savings banks and electronic-money institutions per the 2016 Banking Law's broadened scope.
Financial

BCT Circular 2011-06 and Law 2001-65 amendmentsFinancial Leasing Regulation

Circular: 2011 | Framework updated: 2016 Banking Law
The BCT regulates leasing companies as financial institutions. Capital requirements, prudential ratios, and provisioning rules apply. The 2016 Banking Law (No. 2016-48) updated the regulatory framework for specialized financial institutions including leasing and factoring companies.
Financial

Law No. 76-18 of January 21, 1976 (as amended by Finance Law 2026)Exchange Control Law

Enacted: January 21, 1976 | Amended: multiple times, most recently Finance Law 2026 (Dec. 2025)
Governs foreign exchange transactions, capital movements, and currency restrictions; historically prohibited Tunisian residents from holding foreign currency. A parliamentary amendment to the 2026 Finance Law, adopted December 2, 2025 (69 for/17 against/17 abstain) — after a near-identical proposal was narrowly rejected in November 2024 (51 against/48 for) — for the first time authorizes resident individuals and businesses to open and fund foreign-currency bank accounts, ending a 50-year-old restriction. This 2025 amendment is a stopgap pending the wholesale replacement of the 1976 law by the new Foreign Exchange Code (Draft Law 115/2025) — see separate entry.
FinancialCommercialAmended

Draft Law No. 115/2025 (pending)New Foreign Exchange Code (Code des Changes)

Registered with ARP: 2025 | Finance & Budget Committee hearings: ~90% complete as of June 17, 2026 | Status: not yet enacted as of July 1, 2026
Comprehensive replacement of the 1976 Exchange Control Law and its implementing decrees — the first wholesale rewrite of Tunisia's foreign-exchange regime in 50 years, composed of 91 articles. Would broaden the definition of foreign exchange operations, formally recognize crypto-assets, create a new "licensed exchange operator" status, authorize private exchange offices for the first time, enable a regulated framework for international digital payment platforms, and guarantee immediate and unrestricted repatriation of profits, dividends, and asset-sale proceeds. The Finance and Budget Committee has held extensive hearings (including with CONECT, which submitted 41 proposed amendments, plus the Ministry of Finance and the Directorate General of Customs) and as of mid-June 2026 was weighing whether to proceed to a committee vote without the Central Bank of Tunisia's opinion, after the BCT failed to respond to repeated hearing requests. Update (Run 36): that posture has since softened rather than hardened — a June 23, 2026 report on the committee's continuing work (alongside separate STEG financing-agreement hearings) has Ktari instead calling continued BCT consultation "essential" before final adoption, with the BCT still not having formally responded as of that date; the committee's earlier openness to voting around the BCT's silence should therefore be read as superseded by this more recent, more cautious stance, not as the operative plan. Economists heard by the committee estimated the reform could add 1–1.5 percentage points to GDP growth. Committee chair Maher Ktari has reiterated parliament's aim to pass the bill before the summer 2026 recess, with additional listening sessions continuing into late June before transmission to plenary — the committee also heard the Chamber of Commerce (mixed chambers) on May 14, 2026. However, a July 1, 2026 report on the separate Development Plan debate notes MPs were still publicly urging "acceleration" of this Code's adoption that same day, indicating the bill had not yet passed and Ktari's summer-recess target should be treated as unconfirmed rather than on track. A related but separate track — Bill No. 58/2025 on amnesty for past exchange violations (see entry below) — was sent back to committee in April 2026 with reporting suggesting the two efforts are being harmonized rather than passed separately. Update (Run 70): a July 3, 2026 IACE report named this reform Tunisia's single highest enactment priority among sixteen pending economic reforms, arguing the underlying 1976 exchange-control framework is obsolete against digital-economy payment flows and contemporary cross-border finance; unlike the Investment, Water, Environment, and Hydrocarbons codes IACE separately flagged as stalled pre-ARP transmission (see those entries), this Code is already before the Finance and Budget Committee, so IACE's "priority" framing here concerns urgency of a floor vote rather than executive-stage delay.
FinancialDigital

Bill No. 58/2025 (pending)Regularization ("Amnesty") of Past Exchange-Control Violations

Filed: late May 2025 (Independent National Bloc) | Plenary examination: April 14, 2026 | Sent back to Finance Committee: April 14, 2026 (54 for / 3 against / 2 abstain) | Status: not yet enacted as of July 2026
Distinct from the wholesale Foreign Exchange Code rewrite (Draft Law No. 115/2025, above), this narrower bill would let residents holding undeclared foreign-currency assets or funds held abroad regularize their situation through repatriation and a sworn declaration, in exchange for a one-off "liberatory contribution": reported rates of 15% of value for foreign real estate/financial assets, and 7% of the dinar-equivalent for repatriated sums (reduced to 4% if fully converted to dinars). Payment would extinguish both the underlying exchange-control and tax violations. Brought to a plenary vote on April 14, 2026 after a first committee pass, but following hours of debate and a wave of proposed amendments, deputies voted 54–3–2 to send the text back to the Finance Committee for further work rather than adopting or rejecting it outright — some press coverage described this as the amnesty being "postponed indefinitely," though the bill was not formally withdrawn or defeated. Critics (including deputy Abderrazak Houas) argued the draft was insufficiently rigorous on the source-of-funds and money-laundering questions raised by an amnesty of this kind. Reporting since December 2025 indicates the Finance Committee is now working to harmonize this amnesty mechanism with the broader Foreign Exchange Code reform and Tunisia's anti-money-laundering framework rather than advancing it as a standalone text — watch the Foreign Exchange Code entry above for developments, as the two efforts may ultimately merge.
FinancialTaxPending

Draft Law No. 023/2024 (pending)Combating Financial Exclusion

Filed by the Presidency of the Republic: March 6, 2024 | Referred to Finance & Budget Committee: March 7, 2024 | Committee hearing: June 26, 2026 | Status: not yet enacted as of July 2, 2026
A government-drafted bill to overhaul Tunisia's financial-inclusion framework, developed as the legislative centerpiece of the National Financial Inclusion Strategy. Motivated by a Ministry of Finance diagnosis showing only around 60% of Tunisians over 18 interact with a bank or postal financial institution at all, with far lower uptake of modern financial services (roughly 17% use non-cash payment methods, 3% use mobile financial services, under 2% hold voluntary insurance). Structured around four axes: expanding access to and use of financial services; consolidating sectoral oversight; consumer protection and financial-literacy promotion; and rationalizing financial flows. Correction (Run 43): the "n° 38/2026 and n° 39/2026" numbering attributed to this bill in a prior run was in error — those numbers in fact belong to the unrelated STEG IBRD loan-guarantee bills approved by the same committee two days after this bill's hearing (see standalone entry above). This bill's number is confirmed via the ARP's own official bill registry (arp.tn/loi/project/4016) as Draft Law No. 023/2024 ("مشروع قانون عدد 023 لسنة 2024 يتعلّق بمكافحة الإقصاء المالي"), filed by the Presidency of the Republic on March 6, 2024 and pending in committee since March 7, 2024 — the June 26, 2026 session was a committee hearing on this long-pending 2024 bill, not a newly filed 2026 text. The Finance and Budget Committee hearing was chaired by committee president Maher Ktari with vice-president Dhafer Sghiri and deputies including Amal Moueddeb, Mohamed Ben Hassine, Mustapha Boubakri, and Mohamed Zied El Maher, with officials from the Ministry of Finance, the Microfinance Supervision Authority, and the General Insurance Committee. A central sticking point has been a proposed "banque postale" (postal bank) — now identified as a related but formally separate proposition de loi, n° 82/2025, filed by 21 deputies, which would offer expanded banking-type services through La Poste Tunisienne's network under Central Bank of Tunisia supervision without converting La Poste into a full bank; on June 28, 2026 the Ministry of Finance publicly "temporized" on this specific proposal, describing it as a "strategic option" requiring further legal and economic study and broader stakeholder consensus. Distinct from the pre-existing Microfinance Law (2011) and Banking Law (2016), which the bill would supplement rather than replace. Not yet put to a committee vote or enacted as of this update.
FinancialSocialPending

Proposition de loi n° 82/2025 (pending)Creation of a "Banque Postale" (Postal Bank)

Filed: 2025, by 21 co-sponsoring deputies | Status: not yet enacted as of July 2, 2026
A multi-party member's bill (proposition de loi), distinct from the government's financial-exclusion bill (Draft Law No. 023/2024, above) though addressing an overlapping policy goal, that would authorize La Poste Tunisienne to offer expanded banking-type services — savings, credit, and payment products — through its extensive nationwide branch network under Central Bank of Tunisia supervision, without converting La Poste into a full-fledged licensed bank. Proponents frame it as a financial-inclusion tool reaching underbanked rural and low-income populations already served by La Poste's existing savings-account and payment infrastructure. The Ministry of Finance publicly "temporized" on the proposal on June 28, 2026, describing it as a "strategic option" requiring further legal and economic study rather than something to be resolved within the parallel financial-exclusion bill, signaling likely continued delay. Not yet examined to a committee vote as of this update.
FinancialPending

Law No. 88-108 of August 18, 1988Chartered Accountants (Experts Comptables) (Run 54 addition)

Enacted: August 18, 1988 | Published: JORT No. 56, August 26, 1988 | Superseded: Law No. 82-62 of June 30, 1982
"Portant refonte de la législation relative à la profession d'expert comptable" — creates the Ordre des Experts Comptables de Tunisie (OECT) as a body with legal personality, under Ministry of Finance supervision. Defines the "expert comptable" profession: organizing, verifying, and certifying company accounts, and — subject to conditions — acting as statutory auditor (commissaire aux comptes) referenced in the Companies Code entry above. Sets diploma/qualification requirements for admission to the Order and its disciplinary framework. Wholly absent from this artifact despite 53 prior research runs; verified directly against oect.org.tn.
FinancialAdministrative

Law No. 2002-16 of February 4, 2002Accounting Profession (Compagnie des Comptables de Tunisie) (Run 54 addition)

Enacted: February 4, 2002 | Amended: Law No. 2004-88 of December 31, 2004 (Art. 28 transitional registration deadlines)
"Portant organisation de la profession des comptables" — creates the Compagnie des Comptables de Tunisie, a distinct civil-law professional body (Art. 4) for non-expert accountants performing bookkeeping and general accounting services, who may also perform statutory-auditor functions for commercial companies under specified conditions. Complements, without merging into, the chartered-accountants' Order (OECT, Law No. 88-108, above). Wholly absent from this artifact despite 53 prior research runs.
FinancialAdministrative

Draft Text No. 113/2020 (unpromulgated)Household Over-Indebtedness (Surendettement) (Run 83 addition)

Adopted in principle: Council of Ministers, December 2020 | Status: still unpromulgated as of March 2026 (5+ years)
A wholly new standalone gap identified via a March 2026 IACE report ("Le surendettement des ménages : une urgence nationale," March 27, 2026) and corroborated by L'Economiste Maghrébin and WebManagerCenter (both March 27–28, 2026): Tunisia has no legal definition of household over-indebtedness, no dedicated observatory, no centralized credit bureau, and no specialized adjudication commission, despite household debt reaching roughly TND 32 billion — described as a "critical over-indebtedness threshold." Draft text n°113/2020, adopted in principle by a Council of Ministers in December 2020, would define over-indebtedness as the debtor's manifest inability to honor non-professional debts within a reasonable time, coupled with a good-faith criterion, but remains unpromulgated more than five years later. IACE's 2026 proposal builds on this dormant text with binding prudential debt-to-income ratio caps (40% for consumer credit, 50% for mortgage credit), a per-governorate over-indebtedness commission offering a seven-year recovery plan, partial debt write-off with a reinsertion commitment, or total write-off with a three-year credit ban, plus a national observatory (TND 500,000 first-year budget) and a centralized credit bureau (TND 7 million over three years, funded 50% BCT / 30% international donors / 20% banking sector). None of this has reached the ARP as a filed bill as of this run — the gap is the absence of any consumer-over-indebtedness framework, not a pending-bill status to track under the standard watchlist.
FinancialSocialDraft

Decree No. 2026-148Conditions for "Crédits d'Honneur" (Honor Loans) (Run 547 addition; sharpened Run 569)

Dated: July 23, 2026 | Published: JORT, July 2026 | Legal basis: Article 412 ter (new) of the Commercial Code
Implementing decree fixing the conditions and criteria for granting small, interest-free "crédit d'honneur" financing under Article 412 ter of the Commercial Code, which requires each bank to open a dedicated "compte de la ligne de financement d'honneur" funded from a share of its profits. Ceilings are tiered by beneficiary rather than a flat cap: TND 5,000 for individuals, TND 10,000 for micro-project holders, and TND 25,000 for SMEs and community-based societies (sociétés communautaires) — micro-projects defined as total investment not exceeding TND 150,000, SMEs as TND 150,000–15 million. Loans are interest-free and require no collateral/guarantees, carry a maximum 2-year repayment term with an optional grace period of up to 6 months, and banks must process applications within 10 business banking days and give written reasons for any rejection. At least 50% of each bank's available honor-financing funds must go to SMEs and community societies. Bank auditors (commissaires aux comptes) must file an annual compliance report with the Ministry of Finance and the Central Bank of Tunisia within 3 months, with BCT holding enforcement authority. Complements the pre-existing Microfinance Law (2011) framework by creating a bank-funded, guarantee-free small-credit channel rather than relying solely on licensed microfinance institutions. A wholly new gap identified via a July 25, 2026 La Presse de Tunisie report and independently corroborated by Business News and allAfrica the same day; no prior run had captured Article 412 ter or its implementing decree. (Run 569 update, July 27, 2026) A same-day Managers.tn JORT recap supplied the tiered ceilings, processing deadline, grace period, 50% SME/community-society set-aside, and auditor-reporting mechanics above, replacing the earlier flat-TND-25,000 approximation.
FinancialSocial

📈 Investment & Business Environment

Investment Law — Law No. 2016-71 of September 30, 2016

Enacted: September 30, 2016 | In force: April 2017 | Replaced: Investment Code of 1993
Tunisia's primary investment statute, replacing the 1993 Investment Code. Applies to both domestic and foreign investment. Guarantees freedom of investment, equal treatment of foreign and domestic investors, protection against expropriation (with compensation), and transfer of profits and capital. Establishes the Investment Authority (FIPA — Foreign Investment Promotion Agency) and the Investment Development Agency (IDI). Creates investment incentives including tax benefits for priority sectors and regions. Implementing update: Décret n° 2024-182 of April 4, 2024 (JORT No. 2024-047) amended the executive regulation on financial incentives, Décret gouvernemental n° 2017-389 of March 9, 2017 — not the 2016 law itself — setting a 2-year deadline from the investment declaration certificate date to submit incentive claims with a feasibility study, requiring exceptional deadline-extension requests to be filed 6 months before expiry, and clarifying which agricultural services (milk collection, grain storage, seed conditioning, hay storage) qualify for regional-development incentives.
Key provisions
  • Freedom of establishment for foreign investors (with exceptions in certain sectors)
  • Art. 7: Equal treatment of foreign investors
  • Art. 21–25: Protection against nationalization and expropriation
  • Art. 26: Freedom to transfer capital, profits, and salaries of foreign staff
  • Incentive framework: preferential tax rates for investment in priority sectors
  • Regional development incentives for investments in interior regions
  • Foreign ownership: up to 100% in most sectors; limited in certain strategic sectors
  • Single window (guichet unique) for investment approvals
CommercialFinancial

Free Zones Law — Law No. 92-81 of August 3, 1992

Enacted: August 3, 1992 | In force: 1993 | Amended: 2007, 2009, 2019
Creates special economic zones (free zones) offering customs and tax advantages for export-oriented businesses. Free zone companies are exempt from import duties, VAT on local purchases, and income tax for initial periods. Currently active free zones include Bizerte Economic Activity Park. Requires minimum 70% export of production.
CommercialTax

Startup Act — Law No. 2018-20 of April 17, 2018

Enacted: April 17, 2018 | In force: August 2018
Innovative legislation creating a special legal and fiscal framework for innovative startups. Eligible startups receive a "Startup" label granting advantages including: salary coverage for founders for 1 year, exemption from employer social security contributions for 3 years, preferential tax treatment, facilitated foreign currency access, and streamlined administrative procedures. One of Africa's first dedicated startup laws.
Key provisions
  • "Startup" label granted by the Startup Tunisia Committee
  • Founder salary covered by the state for up to 1 year
  • 3-year exemption from employer social security contributions
  • 5-year corporate tax holiday
  • Foreign currency account allowed for startups
  • Fast-track work permits for foreign talent
  • Crowdfunding and convertible notes facilitated
CommercialDigital

Arbitration Code — Law No. 93-42 of April 26, 1993

Enacted: April 26, 1993 | In force: 1993
Governs domestic and international commercial arbitration in Tunisia. Based on the UNCITRAL Model Law. Provides for arbitration agreements, constitution of tribunals, conduct of proceedings, and recognition and enforcement of awards. Tunisia ratified the New York Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards in 1967.
Commercial

Law No. 2015-49 of November 27, 2015Public-Private Partnership Law

Enacted: November 27, 2015 | In force: 2015
Creates the legal framework for PPP contracts between the state and private operators. Defines PPP modalities: concessions, affermage (lease-operate), and BOT contracts. Establishes the General Committee for Public-Private Partnerships under the Ministry of Finance. Requires competitive tendering and feasibility studies. Enables private participation in infrastructure (ports, airports, highways, water, energy).
CommercialAdministrative

Law No. 92-81 and Investment CodeOffshore (Totally Exporting) Companies Framework

Established: 1992 | Updated: Investment Code 2016
Regime for 100% export-oriented foreign investment: exemption from all customs duties, exemption from VAT on purchases, tax holiday for first 10 years, then 50% reduction. Must export ≥70% of production. May employ up to 30% of workforce from Tunisia. Separately regulated from free zones; offshore companies may locate anywhere in Tunisia.
CommercialTax

Decree-Law No. 2022-15 (as substantially amended by Decree-Law No. 2025-3 of October 3, 2025)Sociétés Communautaires (Community Enterprises)

Original framework: 2022 | Major reform: Decree-Law No. 2025-3, published JORT October 3, 2025
Part of President Kais Saied's "new social project" economic program, this framework creates a distinct legal form — the "community enterprise" — intended as a third pillar of the economy alongside the public and private sectors, oriented toward participatory local and regional economic development. Decree-Law 2025-3 substantially overhauled the original 2022 framework: it distinguishes "local" community enterprises (participants from a single delegation, minimum 10 members) from "regional" ones (participants from multiple delegations within a governorate, minimum 15 members); sets minimum capital at TND 5,000 (local) and TND 10,000 (regional); grants a 10-year total exemption from taxes and duties from formation, VAT suspension on certain operations, preferential bank-loan interest rates, National Guarantee Fund coverage for financing, and priority long-term leasing (up to 40 years, with a 5-year rent exemption) of state-owned agricultural and non-agricultural land; creates a National Register of Community Enterprises conferring legal personality and a unique identifier; and sets governing-board composition at 3–10 members elected for renewable 3-year terms with conflict-of-interest rules. The Finance Law 2025 separately extended VAT-suspension treatment to community enterprises (see Finance Law 2025 entry). (Run 181 addition, corroborated and refined Run 196): a concrete application of this land-leasing mechanism was reported July 6, 2026 — the state released state-owned ("domanial") agricultural land to newly-formed community enterprises, an executive/administrative allocation carried out under this existing decree-law framework rather than a new legislative act. Independently corroborated by a second source (businessnews.com.tn, July 6, 2026), which specifies the allocation as 38 state farms totaling 17,014 hectares, earmarked for priority lease under a November 4, 2025 joint ministerial order implementing this decree-law's land-leasing provisions — resolving Run 181's "single-sourced pending further corroboration" caveat.
CommercialTax

Law No. 89-9 of February 1, 1989Public Enterprises & Privatization Framework

Enacted: February 1, 1989 | Amended: Laws No. 94-102, 96-74, 99-38, 2001-33, 2006-36
Tunisia's foundational statute governing state holdings ("participations"), public enterprises (wholly state-owned companies, and those where the state, local authorities, or public establishments jointly or individually hold over 50%), and public establishments. Defines the legal mechanisms for restructuring these entities — including share sales or exchanges, mergers, divisions, and asset sales — providing the general framework that enables privatization (transfer of state-owned shareholdings to the private sector). Privatization decisions are administered by CAREPP (Commission d'Assainissement et de Restructuration des Entreprises à Participation Publique), created under this law and detailed by Decree No. 97-410. Distinct from the narrower 2011 Confiscation Law entry above, which targets only assets seized from Ben Ali-clan associates via the separate Tunisian Confiscation Commission/Privatisation Authority.
CommercialFinancial

Draft Investment Code Reform Bill (pending)Proposed Overhaul of Law No. 2016-71

Approved in principle: Restricted Ministerial Council, December 31, 2025 | Under committee review: ARP Strategic Planning Commission (article-by-article vote stage) | Status: not enacted as of July 1, 2026
A government-sponsored bill to revise the 2016 Investment Law, launched at a Conseil ministériel restreint on December 31, 2025 and alongside the related Foreign Exchange Code reform (Draft 115/2025), as part of a broader push to modernize Tunisia's business and capital-flow environment. As of late May 2026, the ARP Strategic Planning Commission — chaired by Saber Jelassi — had moved to article-by-article voting after incorporating several amendments, following expert hearings and consultations with business organizations (Conect, Utica, Utap). Goals reported by officials and business groups include streamlining incentive eligibility, easing the single-window approval process, and aligning investment guarantees with the liberalized exchange regime. Reporting as of late June 2026 describes the amended text replacing the current prior-authorization regime with a prior-declaration principle for most investments, coupled with a positive (tacit-approval) administrative-silence rule under strict response deadlines for the administration, plus a proposed "licence premium" fast-track status to attract larger investors; this description was independently repeated by officials at the 22nd Tunisia Investment Forum (June 25–26, 2026), a second source corroborating the reform direction — but none of these provisions could be confirmed against a primary committee text as of this update, so they remain noted as reported reform direction rather than settled article language. Update (Run 38): Tunisian financial press (Tustex, late May/early June 2026) explicitly frames the reform as tied to the 2026–2030 National Development Plan's investment targets, citing a 3.3% GDP growth objective for 2026 specifically and an approximately TND 29.9 billion total public-and-private investment target for the year — figures presented alongside, not yet reconciled against, the Development Plan bill's own five-year targets documented in that entry's standalone card. As of July 1, 2026 the 2016 Investment Law remains unchanged and fully in force; the reform bill had not yet reached a plenary vote. Tracking note: a follow-up search also surfaced a separate, MP-initiated proposition n°26/2024 amending the 2016 law under the same Strategic Planning Commission, distinct from the government-driven track described above; it remains unclear from open sources whether these are the same initiative described differently or two parallel bills before the same commission — flagged for clarification in a future run rather than merged into a single narrative here. Update (Run 70): a July 3, 2026 IACE report ("cartographie" of Tunisia's pending economic-code reforms), independently corroborated by African Manager, La Presse de Tunisie, and Tustex, offers a partial resolution of that ambiguity: it classifies the Investment Code among five priority reforms — alongside the Water, Environment, and Hydrocarbons codes — that are "technically finalized and validated by the Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP," attributing the bottleneck to unfinished interministerial arbitration and sectoral resistance rather than to parliamentary delay. Read together with the ARP Strategic Planning Commission's own article-by-article voting activity documented above, this suggests the government-driven track (approved in principle December 31, 2025) may be the one still stalled pre-transmission, while the committee voting already underway concerns the separate MP-initiated proposition n°26/2024 — a plausible but not yet independently confirmed reconciliation of the two tracks.
