IACE separately named the Investment Code one of its top three enactment priorities for the remainder of 2026, citing a decline in the overall investment rate from 19.3% of GDP (2016) to 16% (2024) and average FDI inflows down 25% (US$728M/year in 2019–2023 vs. US$974M/year in 2014–2018), and recommended a Prime Ministry-based coordination unit with binding milestones to move all sixteen tracked reforms forward. Update (Run 586): a July 4, 2026 La Presse de Tunisie piece ("Créer une entreprise en 24 heures, l'une des principales mesures de la réforme du Code de l'investissement"), framing the reform within the 2026–2030 Development Plan's business-climate agenda, adds previously-uncaptured concrete detail: a headline target of capping business-registration time at 24 hours ("standards internationaux"); a planned national digital single-window investor portal interconnected with public administrative systems to digitize authorization procedures; and digitalization/interconnection of land-registry services with a unified property database for investment-ready land, alongside the already-documented incentive-eligibility, single-window, and exchange-regime-alignment goals. These figures come from secondary press coverage of the Plan rather than a primary committee text, consistent with this entry's existing sourcing caveat, and do not resolve the still-open question of which of the two parallel tracks (government-driven vs. MP-initiated proposition n°26/2024) they attach to.
CommercialFinancial

Law No. 2020-30 of June 30, 2020Social and Solidarity Economy Law (Run 81 addition)

Enacted: June 30, 2020 | Published: JORT N°63, July 3, 2020 (pp. 1399–1403)
A wholly new standalone entry — this artifact's own Run 4 changelog previously described this law as "already implied elsewhere," but a full-text search confirmed no standalone citation for it actually existed; only the distinct 2025 "sociétés communautaires" decree-law (see above) was documented. Confirmed via direct reading of the scanned Journal Officiel text (JORT N°63, hosted by ILO NATLEX). The first framework law of its kind in the Maghreb (Morocco and Algeria had no equivalent text at the time of enactment, per leaders.com.tn), giving legal status and a governance structure to the "économie sociale et solidaire." Article 2 defines the sector as an economic model of social-purpose activities whose primary goal is not profit distribution; Article 3 lists covered entity types — cooperatives, agricultural/fisheries development groups, mutual associations, microfinance associations, mutual insurance companies, Decree-Law 2011-88 associations conducting social-purpose economic activity, and certain qualifying companies/economic-interest groupings. Creates a Conseil supérieur de l'économie sociale et solidaire (Art. 7) and an Instance tunisienne de l'économie sociale et solidaire (Art. 8). Secondary sourcing indicates the law remained largely unimplemented roughly ten months after passage pending its application decrees — an implementation gap, not a citation issue.
CommercialSocial

💡 Intellectual Property Law

Law No. 2009-33 of June 23, 2009Literary and Artistic Property (Copyright)

Enacted: June 23, 2009 | In force: 2009 | Replaced: Law No. 94-36
Governs copyright and related rights in Tunisia. Protects literary, artistic, and musical works, software, databases, and audiovisual productions. Protection term: author's life + 70 years. Covers moral rights (inalienable) and economic rights (transferable). Aligns with WIPO Copyright Treaty and TRIPS Agreement obligations. Enforced by the Tunisian Copyright Protection Office (OTPDA). Pending reform: Bill Proposal No. 2024-47, amending and supplementing the copyright framework to address digital-era exploitation of works, is under review by the ARP's Rights and Freedoms Committee; a June 24, 2026 parliamentary study day examined the draft's likely impact on the protection regime. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–10: Scope — literary, artistic, musical, audiovisual, photographic works; software
  • Art. 15: Moral rights (right of integrity, right of attribution) — inalienable and perpetual
  • Art. 16–22: Economic rights — reproduction, distribution, public performance
  • Art. 24: Duration — life of author + 70 years
  • Art. 45–55: Related rights for performers, producers, broadcasters
  • Art. 60–75: Exceptions and limitations (private use, education, libraries)
  • Art. 87–100: Civil and criminal remedies for infringement
IP

Law No. 2000-84 of August 24, 2000Industrial Property (Patents, Trademarks, Designs)

Enacted: August 24, 2000 | In force: 2001 | Amended: 2007
Governs patents, trademarks, geographical indications, industrial designs, and utility models in Tunisia. Administered by the Tunisian Intellectual Property Office (INNORPI). Tunisia is a member of WIPO, the Paris Convention, the Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT), and the Madrid Protocol on trademarks.
Key provisions
  • Patents: 20-year protection from filing date; requires novelty, inventive step, industrial application
  • Trademarks: 10-year protection, renewable; registration at INNORPI required
  • Industrial designs: 5-year protection, renewable up to 4 times (25 years total)
  • Geographical indications: protection for Tunisian products with geographic origin
  • Compulsory licenses: available for public health or national emergency
IP

Law No. 99-42 of May 10, 1999Plant Variety Protection

Enacted: May 10, 1999 | In force: 1999
Provides plant breeders' rights (PBR) over new plant varieties. Tunisia is a member of the International Union for the Protection of New Varieties of Plants (UPOV). Protection term: 20 years (25 years for vines and trees). Administered jointly by INNORPI and the Ministry of Agriculture. Protects commercially registered varieties from unauthorized reproduction, conditioning, sale, or export.
IPAgriculture

COC Arts. 83–100 and Decree No. 2018-417Trade Secret and Unfair Competition

COC base: 1906 | Regulatory update: 2018
Tunisian law protects trade secrets through COC provisions on unjust enrichment and breach of confidence. The 2018 regulatory decree reinforces protection against industrial espionage and unauthorized disclosure of commercial secrets. Penal Code Art. 254 criminalizes breach of professional secrecy. Unfair competition (dénigrement, parasitism, misleading origin) actionable under COC Art. 83 and the Competition Law.
IPCommercial

Law No. 2001-21 of February 6, 2001Protection of Semiconductor Topographies

Enacted: February 6, 2001 | In force: February 2001
Protects original topographies (layout designs) of integrated circuits (semiconductors) against unauthorized copying and commercial exploitation. Implements Tunisia's obligations under the TRIPS Agreement (WTO, Art. 35–38) and the Washington Treaty on Intellectual Property in Respect of Integrated Circuits (1989). Protection lasts 10 years from the date of first commercial exploitation or 15 years from creation, whichever ends first. Registration with INNORPI (Institut National de la Normalisation et de la Propriété Industrielle) is required for protection. Reverse engineering and independent creation are permitted; exhaustion of rights applies to legitimately marketed products.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–5: Definitions: topography, integrated circuit, layout design
  • Art. 6–10: Originality requirement; rights conferred on creator
  • Art. 11–15: Registration with INNORPI; filing requirements
  • Art. 16–20: Duration of protection (10 years from first commercial use)
  • Art. 21–26: Permitted acts: private use, research, teaching, reverse engineering
  • Art. 27–32: Infringement remedies: civil damages, seizure, destruction of infringing articles
  • Art. 33–36: Criminal sanctions: up to 2 years + fines for willful infringement
IP

Law No. 2009-38 of June 30, 2009National Standardization System

Enacted: June 30, 2009 | Repealed: Law No. 82-66 of August 6, 1982
Establishes the general legal framework for standardization in Tunisia, distinct from the trademark/patent registration functions covered by the Industrial Property Law above, though both are administered by the same institute. Confirms the National Institute of Standardization and Industrial Property (INNORPI — Institut National de la Normalisation et de la Propriété Industrielle) as the body responsible for managing the national standardization system: developing, publishing, and periodically revising Tunisian standards (normes tunisiennes) through a consensus-based process grounded in impartiality and independence, and providing normative monitoring (veille normative) to inform economic operators and the public of applicable standards and technical documents. States standardization's policy purposes — strengthening the national economy, facilitating trade, improving product/service quality and competitiveness, protecting consumer health and safety, protecting the environment, and supporting sustainable development. Tunisian standards are voluntary by default, but all or part of a standard can be rendered mandatory by a joint order of the minister in charge of industry and the sector-competent minister (technical regulations); Art. 8 provides for temporary exemptions from mandatory-standard compliance where an operator faces genuine difficulty applying it.
IPCommercial

Law No. 99-40 of May 10, 1999Legal Metrology (as amended by Law No. 2008-12 of February 11, 2008)

Enacted: May 10, 1999 | Amended: February 11, 2008
Distinct from, and predates, the Standardization Law above: a 45-article, 7-chapter statute establishing Tunisia's legal-units-of-measurement framework, metrological control over measuring instruments (approval, verification, stamping), and a National Council of Legal Metrology, plus rules governing the sale, possession, and use of measuring instruments in trade. The 2008 amendment (Art. 15 bis) created the Agence Nationale de Métrologie (ANM), operational from January 1, 2009, which now administers legal, industrial, and scientific metrology together — distinct from INNORPI's standardization and industrial-property functions above, though both institutes coordinate on technical-regulation matters.
IPCommercial

🌿 Environmental Law

Law No. 88-91 of August 2, 1988Framework Environmental Law

Enacted: August 2, 1988 | In force: 1988 | Amended: 1992, 2001
Tunisia's foundational environmental statute, creating the National Environment Protection Agency (ANPE). Establishes environmental impact assessment (EIA) requirements for major projects. Governs pollution prevention, waste management, and natural resource protection. Establishes the principle of "polluter pays." Pending consolidation (added Run 73): the Ministry of Environment has been drafting a wholesale replacement — a unified "Code de l'Environnement" of roughly 400 articles consolidating this framework law with the Waste Management, Marine Pollution, EIA-decree, and related sectoral texts below — described by the Minister as fully rewritten and finalized (environnement.gov.tn; TAP, July 2026). A July 3, 2026 IACE report on stalled economic reforms lists this Environment Code, alongside the Investment, Water, and Hydrocarbons codes (see those entries), as "technically finalized and validated by the Council of Ministers" but "never transmitted to the ARP" — an executive-stage bottleneck shared with the other three. No draft article text or ARP registry number has been published; Law No. 88-91 remains the operative statute pending transmission.
Environmental

Water Code — Law No. 75-16 of March 31, 1975

Enacted: March 31, 1975 | In force: 1975 | Amended: 1987, 2001
Classifies all water resources (surface and groundwater) as public domain. Governs water use permits, irrigation rights, water conservation, and protection from pollution. Establishes the National Water Authority and regional water committees. The 2022 Constitution (Art. 48) enshrines the right to safe drinking water as a fundamental entitlement. Emergency water restriction orders issued in 2023 following severe drought; a summer-2026 emergency water program (81 projects, TND 58 million) was launched to secure supply. Reported pending reform (unconfirmed): government officials referenced preparation of a new, comprehensive "Code de l'eau" as of early 2025; reported structure would comprise seven chapters (guiding principles; identification and preservation of the water public domain; governance; use and exploitation; water-service provision; risk management; and criminal protections). Update (Run 51): the source and date for this reported structure are now precisely identified — Agriculture, Water Resources & Fisheries Minister Ezzeddine Ben Cheikh presented the draft to a Conseil ministériel restreint on February 25, 2025, with the ministry instructed to amend it in coordination with the government's legal and legislative advisory services before finalizing a version for submission to the Council of Ministers and then parliament (La Presse de Tunisie, Webmanagercenter, L'Economiste Maghrébin, allAfrica, all dated March 11, 2025). A single, separate and still-uncorroborated report describes the draft as having since been submitted to the Prime Ministry (Présidence du Gouvernement) for review ahead of that transmission, but this remains unconfirmed by a second source or a confirmed date. No public draft text or enactment had been confirmed as of mid-2026, and the 1975 Code remains in force. What has been confirmed and enacted, distinct from the rumored wholesale replacement, is an incremental amendment: Article 65 of the Finance Law 2026 created a "Fonds de l'Eau" (Water Fund) financed by a tariff surcharge on bottled-water companies — a targeted funding mechanism layered onto the existing 1975 framework rather than a new code. Update (Run 70): a July 3, 2026 IACE report, corroborated across African Manager, La Presse de Tunisie, and Tustex, sharpens the "submitted to the Prime Ministry" claim considerably: it states the new Water Code is one of five pending codes (with Investment, Environment, and Hydrocarbons) "technically finalized and validated by the Council of Ministers" but "never transmitted to the ARP" — a specific, now multiply-sourced procedural status, though still short of a primary government or JORT confirmation naming this Water Code draft by number or article count.
Environmental

Forestry Code — Law No. 88-20 of April 13, 1988

Enacted: April 13, 1988 (JORT No. 25 of April 15, 1988) | In force: 1988
Classifies and protects Tunisian forests and natural parks. Regulates logging permits, forest fires (criminalized), afforestation, and pastoral use of forest land. Creates the Directorate General of Forests under the Ministry of Agriculture. Hunting is not governed by a separate statute: Title II ("De la chasse," Arts. 165, 193–194) of this Code regulates hunting rights, permits, and closed seasons, with annual species/zone quotas set by ministerial arrêté (confirmed Run 145 — no standalone hunting law exists). Pending reform advocacy (Run 112): a January 2026 FTDES report describes the code as unreformed since 1988 and calls for a dedicated "Forests and Climate" chapter to unlock REDD+ climate-financing mechanisms; no bill or Council of Ministers text has been filed as of mid-2026.
Environmental

Waste Management Law — Law No. 96-41 of June 10, 1996

Enacted: June 10, 1996 | In force: 1996 | Amended: 2007
Governs waste prevention, collection, transport, treatment, and disposal. Creates the National Agency for Waste Management (ANGed). Establishes the waste hierarchy (prevention, reuse, recycling, recovery, disposal). Special provisions for hazardous waste, medical waste, and industrial effluents.
Environmental

National Sanitation Office (ONAS) — Law No. 74-73 of August 3, 1974

Enacted: August 3, 1974 | Amended: Law No. 93-41 of April 19, 1993 | In force: ongoing
Creates the Office National de l'Assainissement (ONAS), the public utility responsible for wastewater collection, treatment, and sanitation infrastructure across Tunisia's urban network. Law No. 93-41 (1993) expanded ONAS's originally urban-only mandate to industrial-effluent pretreatment oversight and broader rural/peri-urban sanitation coverage, and strengthened its tariff-setting and financing authority (the sanitation surcharge collected via SONEDE water bills). ONAS operates alongside SONEDE (drinking-water distribution) and ANPE/the Ministry of Environment (pollution enforcement — see the Framework Environmental Law and Marine Pollution entries above) within Tunisia's water-sector regulatory architecture. (Added Run 82 — a genuine standalone gap outside the pending-bill watchlist: the sanitation utility's own governing statute, distinct from the Water Code and Marine Pollution Law, had been absent from this document through 81 prior runs.)
EnvironmentalAdministrative

Law No. 2017-49 of July 3, 2017Paris Agreement Ratification

Ratified: July 3, 2017 | COP21 Agreement: December 12, 2015
Tunisia ratified the Paris Agreement on Climate Change. Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) commits Tunisia to a 41% reduction in carbon intensity by 2030 (conditional on international support). Updated NDC submitted in 2021 with binding targets for renewable energy, energy efficiency, and transport emissions. Tunisia also ratified the Kyoto Protocol (2002) and the Vienna Convention on ozone depletion. NDC 3.0 (2026 update): Tunisia published its third NDC update in late April 2026 and presented it to over 200 non-state actors at a Gammarth seminar on May 16–17, 2026. NDC 3.0 raises the carbon-intensity reduction target to 62% by 2035 (from a 46.4%-by-2030 trajectory), against the 2010 baseline, and estimates financing needs of roughly US$55 billion for 2026–2035 (about 53% for adaptation — led by water and food security — and 47% for mitigation). It builds on a national climate finance taxonomy adopted in 2025 and is intended to be integrated into the state budget and the 2026–2030 national development plan rather than implemented as standalone legislation.
EnvironmentalConstitutional

Law No. 95-73 of July 24, 1995Maritime Public Domain and Coastal Protection

Enacted: July 24, 1995 | In force: 1995 | Decree No. 2004-2727 (implementing)
Defines and protects the maritime public domain (50 metres from high-tide line). No construction permitted without prior authorization from APAL (Agency for Coastal Protection and Planning — Agence de Protection et d'Aménagement du Littoral). Governs beach access, coastal setbacks, and seaside urbanization restrictions. Strengthened environmental impact controls on port and tourist developments along coastlines. (Added Run 312) APAL official Mehdi Belhaj announced July 14, 2026 a free hotline (1820) via the "Al Jisr" platform for reporting maritime-public-domain violations (illegal beach occupation, abusive pricing, anarchic exploitation, and pollution) — APAL recorded 106 such infractions the preceding week alone — and stated that a new legal framework specifically targeting pollution-related infractions, with sanctions ranging from fines to imprisonment depending on severity (particular focus on industrial pollution requiring wastewater-treatment installations), would take effect "soon"; no bill number, draft text, or committee referral has yet been identified for this framework, so it is noted here as an implementation-level lead rather than a standalone pending-bill entry.
EnvironmentalLand

Decree No. 2005-1991 of July 11, 2005Environmental Impact Assessment

Enacted: July 11, 2005 | In force: 2005 | Amended: 2012
Requires Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) studies for listed major projects (Annex I: mandatory EIA; Annex II: simplified environmental notice). Covered projects include industrial plants, mining operations, tourism facilities, infrastructure, and large-scale agriculture. ANPE reviews EIA dossiers and issues environmental authorization before the building permit. Public consultation required for Annex I projects.
EnvironmentalAdministrative

Law No. 96-29 of June 3, 1996Marine Pollution Prevention and Control

Enacted: June 3, 1996 | In force: 1996
Tunisia's primary statute on marine environmental protection and pollution prevention along its 1,300 km Mediterranean coastline. Prohibits the discharge of hydrocarbons, sewage, waste, and noxious substances into Tunisian territorial waters and the exclusive economic zone. Establishes emergency response obligations for ships and port operators in case of accidental spills. Implements MARPOL 73/78 (the International Convention for the Prevention of Pollution from Ships) obligations in domestic law. Assigns the Merchant Marine Directorate and the National Guard (maritime units) enforcement responsibilities. Criminal sanctions: up to 5 years imprisonment and significant fines for deliberate discharges.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–6: Scope — territorial sea (12 nm), contiguous zone (24 nm), and EEZ (200 nm)
  • Art. 7–15: Prohibition of discharge of hydrocarbons and harmful substances; exceptions
  • Art. 16–22: Ship waste reception facilities in ports; waste manifests
  • Art. 23–28: Emergency response plans (POLMAR plans) for port operators and major vessel traffic
  • Art. 29–35: Aerial and maritime surveillance; inspection of vessels
  • Art. 36–42: Enforcement: confiscation of vessels; criminal sanctions for ship masters
  • Art. 43–48: Coastal industrial discharge limits; ANPE monitoring of industrial outfalls
EnvironmentalTransport

Proposition de loi n° 33/2026 (pending)Draft Law on Reducing Single-Use Plastic Bag Consumption

Filed by 25 ARP deputies, reported: July 5, 2026 | Committee hearing: June 25, 2026 | Status: Industry/Environment Committee review, no floor vote
Proposition de loi n° 33/2026, filed by 25 ARP deputies, that would mandate a progressive phase-out of single-use plastic bags in favor of reusable or biodegradable alternatives, and set technical standards to ensure the quality and effectiveness of those substitutes. Cites Tunisia's estimated 4.2 billion single-use plastic bags consumed annually (roughly 400 per resident), generating about 188,000 tonnes of plastic waste, of which some 60% is improperly managed — distinct from, and narrower than, the still-untransmitted "Code de l'Environnement" consolidation project noted in the Framework Environmental Law entry above. (Added Run 109 — a wholly new standalone entry outside the pending-bill watchlist, filed the same day as that run's research cutoff.) (Run 132 refinement) Multiply-sourced detail (La Presse, businessnews.com.tn, Tunisie Numérique, Le Temps, African Manager, WebManagerCenter, all July 5, 2026): the bill would give producers/importers/distributors 12 months after JORT publication to phase out single-use bags entirely; proposes fines of 5,000–50,000 TND for continued production, import, or sale (doubling on repeat offenses, with possible business closure as an aggravated penalty) and 6 months' to 2 years' imprisonment for illegal production or import. (Run 475 sync fix) The bill number (confirmed at Run 110 as n° 33/2026) and its actual committee status — an Industry/Environment Committee hearing of the initiating deputies on June 25, 2026, predating the July 5 press coverage, per Run 110's finding and reconfirmed unchanged through Run 471 — had been established in this changelog for over 360 runs but never carried into this card, which still read "newly submitted, not yet referred to committee"; corrected here to match.
EnvironmentalPending

Proposition de loi n° 118/2025 (pending)Posidonia (Seagrass) Protection Bill

Committee hearing: June 25, 2026 (Industry, Commerce, Natural Resources, Energy & Environment Committee, chaired by Mohamed Amine Mbareki) | Status: committee review, no floor vote
Proposition de loi n° 118/2025 to protect Tunisia's Posidonia oceanica seagrass meadows, heard by the same Industry/Commerce/Environment Committee session as the single-use plastic bags bill above on June 25, 2026 — a genuine gap this pass, entirely absent from 480 prior runs despite that shared hearing date. Framed around the plant's ecological role in coastal-erosion prevention, carbon storage, water-quality preservation, and marine biodiversity (Tunisia holds a significant share of the Mediterranean's Posidonia coverage), and around a blue-carbon economic rationale: monetizing the associated carbon stock through carbon-credit mechanisms to mobilize additional state financing under green-economy frameworks. The initiative corresponds to the legislative component of "PENELOPE," a project launched by the French Facility for Global Environment (FFEM) and WWF France to protect Posidonia meadows and develop blue-carbon financing in Tunisia. Deputies requested further expert and civil-society input during the June 25 hearing. Not yet enacted as of this pass. (Added Run 481.)
EnvironmentalPending

📰 Press, Media & Freedom of Expression

Decree-Law No. 2011-115 of November 2, 2011Press and Publication Code

Enacted: November 2, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Replaced: Press Code of 1975
Post-revolution statute guaranteeing freedom of the press and abolishing prior government control over media. Abolished the requirement for prior authorization to publish newspapers. Criminalized defamation but with strong protections for press freedom. Created the High Authority for Audio-Visual Communication (HAICA — Haute Autorité Indépendante de la Communication Audiovisuelle).
Press

Decree-Law No. 2011-116 of November 2, 2011HAICA (Audio-Visual Regulator)

Enacted: November 2, 2011 | In force: 2011
Establishes the High Authority for Audio-Visual Communication (HAICA) as an independent regulatory body for radio and television broadcasting. HAICA grants broadcasting licenses, sets content standards, and monitors compliance. Replaced the previous state-controlled broadcasting authority.
Press

Law No. 90-49 of May 7, 1990National Broadcasting Establishment (ERTT)

Enacted: May 7, 1990 | Predecessor: RTT (created by decree, April 25, 1957) | Split into separate radio/TV establishments: 2007
Converted Radio Télévision Tunisienne (RTT) — previously a service administratively and financially dependent on the Ministry of Information — into the Établissement de la Radiodiffusion-Télévision Tunisienne (ERTT), a public establishment with administrative and financial autonomy operating national radio and television. Following a November 2006 presidential announcement, ERTT was split, effective August 31, 2007, into separate Établissement de la Radio Tunisienne and Établissement de la Télévision Tunisienne entities. Governs only the public broadcaster's own corporate/administrative status — sector-wide licensing and content-standard oversight sits with HAICA below (and, if enacted, its proposed replacement). (Run 90 addition)
PressAdministrative

Organic Law No. 2016-22 of March 24, 2016Access to Information

Enacted: March 24, 2016 | In force: 2017 | Enforcement authority dissolved: August 2025
Comprehensive right-to-information law creating an independent National Authority for Access to Information (INAI — Instance Nationale d'Accès à l'Information). Any person may request information from public bodies; response within 20 days required. Narrow exceptions for national security, privacy, and commercial confidentiality. Considered one of the strongest RTI laws in the MENA region when enacted. Status as of 2026: Tunisian authorities dissolved INAI in early August 2025, eliminating the independent body responsible for enforcing access-to-information requests and adjudicating appeals against public-body refusals. ARTICLE19 and the IDEA Global State of Democracy tracker both logged the move as removing the last independent oversight mechanism created during the post-2011 transition; rights groups and Tunisian civil society condemned the dissolution as a further blow to transparency. The underlying statutory right to request information nominally remains in the text of Organic Law No. 2016-22, but without a functioning independent authority to enforce it.
PressAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-54 of September 13, 2022Combating Information Technology Offenses

Enacted: September 13, 2022 | In force: September 2022 | Still in active use as of 2026
Criminalizes "false information" spread online that "harms third parties, public order, or national defense" (Art. 24: up to 5 years imprisonment and TND 50,000 fine, doubled for statements about public officials). Criticized by RSF, CPJ, Amnesty International, Access Now, and ICJ for vague language enabling prosecution of journalists, bloggers, and critics. Passed by presidential decree-law without parliamentary scrutiny. New prosecutions of journalists and online activists were brought in January 2026 — including radio hosts Mourad Zeghidi and Borhen Bsaies, each sentenced to a further 3.5 years on January 22, 2026, partly on related money-laundering charges illustrating a pattern of layered prosecutions. Their appeal proceeded through 2026 with release requests repeatedly rejected by the Tunis Court of Appeal (April 14 and April 28, 2026), keeping both journalists in detention since May 11, 2024; RSF's retrospective (published mid-2026) frames the case as running "from Decree-Law 54 to money laundering" over two years of what it calls judicial harassment. CPJ reported in May 2026 on additional measures beyond Decree 54 used to pressure the press. RSF's May 2026 World Press Freedom Index ranked Tunisia 137th globally amid what the organization called a 25-year global low for press freedom. Courts have at times narrowed the decree's application: on February 3, 2025, the Cour de Cassation's Criminal Chamber 29 ruled, in the case of lawyer/journalist Sonia Dahmani, that the decree's scope does not extend to opinions expressed by journalists in the written or audiovisual press, limiting it to offenses committed through or using information/communication systems — though lower courts have not consistently followed this precedent, and Dahmani was nonetheless convicted and sentenced to 18 months in a separate Decree 54 case in April 2026. Prosecutions continued into mid-2026: in June 2026, Tumedia director and journalist Khaoula Boukrim was sentenced to four years' imprisonment over Facebook posts critical of public policy, prompting condemnation from the National Union of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT); the Committee for Justice separately reported further cumulative prison sentences against Dahmani in unrelated cases during the same period. As of 2026, no legislative review or repeal has occurred, though the ARP's General Legislation Commission announced in December 2025 that it would examine amendment bills to the decree during 2026, with hearings planned for journalists' and lawyers' unions. (Added Run 173): CPJ's May 2026 "Beyond Decree 54" report, cited above, documents that pressure on the press increasingly bypasses this decree's own criminal provisions in favor of administrative and associational tools — banking freezes, tax audits, and outright judicial dissolution of a media outlet's publishing association — exemplified by the Al Khatt/Inkyfada dissolution proceeding; see the Administrative Suspensions & Judicial Dissolution Proceedings entry for full detail. (Run 481 enrichment): the amendment attempt's full documented history, previously only summarized as "announced in December 2025": originally filed February 20, 2024 but never transmitted to committee, then resubmitted by 60 deputies in January 2025; General Legislation Committee hearings opened July 2, 2025 under then-chair Yassine Kourrari, hearing the bill's initiating deputies first, and targeted revisions to Articles 5, 6, 9, 10, 11, and 12 plus the outright repeal of Article 24 itself — the article underlying the vast majority of prosecutions documented above — framed as necessary to align the decree with the 2022 Constitution and the Budapest Convention on Cybercrime (ratified by Tunisia in 2024, see the Cybercrime Convention accession entry). Review continued under new committee chair Faouzi Daâs, who placed the revision atop the committee's agenda from January 2026; a further hearing on March 27, 2026 took testimony from the National Bar Association (ONAT) favoring a comprehensive overhaul, but as of the most recent reporting found (March 28, 2026) the outcome remained explicitly "uncertain," and no committee report, plenary vote, or amendment had followed through this pass — over sixteen months after the first hearing.
PressCriminalDigital

Ghassen Ben Khelifa ProsecutionDecree-Law No. 2022-54 — Inhiyez Editor-in-Chief

Case opened, 5-day detention: September 2022 | Convicted, Tunis Court of First Instance: March 27, 2026 (2 years) | Departed Tunisia: March 30, 2026
Ghassen Ben Khelifa, editor-in-chief of the news site Inhiyez, was sentenced on March 27, 2026 to two years' imprisonment under Decree-Law No. 2022-54, over allegations that he managed a Facebook page accused of spreading "false information" harmful to public security — a case first opened over three years earlier, in September 2022, when he was held for five days before release pending investigation. Ben Khelifa denies any connection to the page, calls the case fabricated, and left Tunisia three days after the verdict. The National Syndicate of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT) said no material or technical evidence, including expert forensic findings, substantiated the charge; the International Federation of Journalists (IFJ) and the Committee for Justice (CFJ) both condemned the conviction as a political prosecution and called for the charges to be dropped. A genuinely previously-undocumented case despite falling squarely within this artifact's already-thorough coverage of the Decree-Law 54 press-prosecution pattern (Zeghidi/Bsaies, Dahmani, Boukrim — all cross-referenced above); confirmed via Business News (two independent March 30 and March 31, 2026 reports), Kapitalis (three independent reports), IFJ, and the Committee for Justice, cross-checked against each other. (Added Run 581)
PressCriminalDigital2026

Decree-Law No. 2022-17 of March 10, 2022HAICA Amendment

Enacted: March 10, 2022 | In force: 2022
Amended HAICA's organic statute under post-July 2021 decree-law powers. Modified the appointment procedure for HAICA members, increasing presidential influence over the regulator. Criticized by media freedom organizations and the Venice Commission for weakening HAICA's independence. HAICA continued operations but faced political pressure over license renewals for channels critical of the government.
PressConstitutional

Draft Organic Law on Audiovisual Communication (pending, filed February 25, 2025)Proposed Replacement of HAICA

Filed: February 25, 2025, by 16 co-sponsoring deputies | Under review: ARP Rights & Freedoms Commission | Status: not yet enacted as of July 1, 2026
A 99-article draft organic law across five chapters — organized around sector principles, the organization and functioning of a new regulatory authority, organization of the audiovisual media sector, offenses and sanctions, and final/transitional provisions — that would repeal Decree-Laws No. 2011-115 and No. 2011-116 and replace HAICA entirely with a new permanent regulatory instance. Sponsors argue the change is needed because HAICA, as constituted, is not a constitutional body under the 2022 Constitution and its members' activity has effectively been frozen. As of May 20, 2026, the ARP Rights and Freedoms Commission was still debating which committee holds proper jurisdiction over the bill — General Legislation, which received the original April 2025 referral, had asked Rights and Freedoms for an advisory opinion — before substantive examination could begin; Commission vice-president Aymen Ben Salah subsequently announced that hearings on the bill were planned to begin in June 2026, though no independent confirmation that those hearings occurred or concluded was found as of this update. Update: at its June 4, 2026 meeting, the ARP Bureau reaffirmed its original April 10, 2025 ruling — the bill (numbered 14/2025 in this source) stays with the Commission de Législation Générale as lead committee, with Rights and Freedoms limited to an advisory opinion — resolving the jurisdictional question in the same direction as before rather than newly, and no confirmation of substantive hearings on the bill's content has surfaced. Media professionals and press-freedom groups have called for a new independent authority but disagree over the bill's specific design. Not yet enacted; HAICA (as amended by Decree-Law No. 2022-17) remains the operative regulator.
PressPending

💻 Digital Law & ICT

Law No. 2004-63 of July 27, 2004 (organic reform bill pending, 2025–2026)Personal Data Protection

Enacted: July 27, 2004 | In force: 2004 | Amended: 2013 | Reform bill: introduced 2025, under ARP Rights & Freedoms Committee review as of 2026
Tunisia's data protection statute — one of the first in Africa. Creates the National Authority for Personal Data Protection (INPDP — Instance Nationale de Protection des Données Personnelles). Requires prior authorization from INPDP before processing sensitive personal data. Establishes data subject rights (access, rectification, deletion) and obligations on data controllers. A comprehensive Fundamental Bill on the Protection of Personal Data — Proposition n°95/2025, filed by 17 deputies per the ARP's own bill tracker (referred to committee July 16, 2025) — is pending before the Assembly of People's Representatives' Rights and Freedoms Committee. The bill comprises 132 articles across six titles (general provisions; processing principles; data-subject rights; processing systems; a new Personal Data Protection Authority replacing INPDP; penalties/transitional measures), per the ARP tracker, Espace Manager, and Nessma, which independently corroborate 132; a "123 articles" figure circulating in some secondary summaries appears to conflate the bill's article count with Article 123 of the ARP's internal rules of procedure (the provision under which the bill was referred to committee) — treat 132 as the correct figure absent a primary JORT text. The bill aligns Tunisian law with the EU GDPR and Council of Europe Convention 108+, addressing biometric data, facial recognition, and algorithmic profiling, and introducing GDPR-style turnover-based administrative fines. Adoption is partly aimed at securing EU "adequacy" status for cross-border data transfers. Committee sessions ran February–May 2026, then June 3 and June 25, 2026 (most recent as of this run); as of the June 3 session the committee was still awaiting written opinions from several ministries and had not yet scheduled civil-society hearings — no committee report or vote as of July 1, 2026. Not yet enacted; the 2004 law remains in force. Update (Run 123): a November 30, 2025 presidency announcement (reported December 1, 2025) disclosed that INPDP's own governing structure — an enlarged 15-member council serving three-year terms — has sat without a renewed mandate since it expired April 16, 2024, marginalizing the authority's oversight capacity through 2023–2026; the government separately confirmed the 2004 law is considered "obsolete" in part because it still names a representative of the now-dissolved Chamber of Advisors. This executive-branch institutional-reform track is distinct from, though aimed at the same underlying problem as, the ARP's own committee review of Proposition n°95/2025 above. AI governance note: Tunisia has no dedicated artificial-intelligence statute; the 2026–2030 National Development Plan references completing an executive plan for a national AI strategy (building on an initial 2018 AI strategy) and launching its implementation, but as of mid-2026 this remains a policy-level initiative without an identified decree or law number — AI-specific processing (profiling, biometric/facial recognition) would instead fall under this pending Personal Data Protection bill once enacted. Update (Run 582): external corroboration of the same governance gap: a July 13, 2026 Business News report ("La Tunisie 110e sur 135 au classement mondial de l'IA responsable") placed Tunisia 110th of 135 countries on the Global Index on Responsible AI 2026 (score 16.37/100), attributing the low ranking in part to the continued absence of a binding national AI legal framework — consistent with, not a change to, the AI-governance status already documented here. A same-run sweep of the standing near-term watchlist (Foreign Exchange Code, Mseddi/Ennahdha designation bill, CPG/GCT financing-guarantee bills, LTDH suspension appeal, ONAT general assembly) found no change from Run 581.
Key provisions
  • Mandatory registration of personal data processing with INPDP
  • Prior authorization for sensitive data (health, biometric, political, religious)
  • Data subject rights: access, rectification, objection, deletion
  • Restrictions on cross-border data transfers
  • Security obligations for data controllers
  • Criminal sanctions for violations: up to 2 years + fines
Digital

Law No. 99-89 of August 2, 1999 (as amended)Cybercrime Law

Enacted: August 2, 1999 | In force: 1999 | Amended: 2004, 2010
Criminalizes unauthorized access to computer systems, interception of electronic communications, introduction of malicious code, and computer fraud. Establishes penalties of 2 to 5 years imprisonment for basic offenses; up to 8 years for attacks on critical infrastructure. Pre-dates the current Digital Code effort.
DigitalCriminal

Telecommunications Code — Law No. 2001-1 of January 15, 2001

Enacted: January 15, 2001 | In force: 2001 | Amended: 2002, 2008, 2013
Liberalizes the telecommunications sector and establishes the independent regulator (INT — Instance Nationale des Télécommunications). Governs licensing of operators, frequency management, interconnection, universal service obligations, and quality standards. Opened mobile and internet markets to competition. Article 86 — criminalizing "knowingly harming others or disturbing their peace" via public telecommunications networks, up to 2 years' imprisonment — has been repeatedly used against journalists and commentators alongside Decree-Law No. 2022-54 (see Criminal Law section). Update (Run 63): journalist Zied El Heni was sentenced to one year in prison under Article 86 on May 7, 2026 (arrested April 24, 2026 after sharing a social-media post criticizing the treatment of journalist Khalifa Guesmi); the Tunis Court of Appeal confirmed the sentence on June 26, 2026. Reporters Without Borders (RSF), the International Federation of Journalists, and the Committee for Justice condemned the ruling as an abusive use of the telecom-harassment provision to suppress press criticism. Update (Run 182): former deputy Rached Khiari was also convicted under Article 86, over a Facebook publication alleging "disclosure of confidential documents"; his first-instance 8-month sentence was reduced to 6 months on appeal by the Tunis Court of Appeal's correctional chamber on April 10, 2026. Rights group CFJ (Committee for Justice) condemned the sentence as political and separately raised concern for Khiari's health, reporting he had been released from an earlier detention in grave condition with chest tumors and alleging inadequate medical care while detained (CFJ, Kapitalis, L'Économiste Maghrébin). Update (Run 240): Zied El Heni faces a second, legally distinct prosecution — not under Article 86 but under the Anti-Terrorism Law (Organic Law No. 2015-26, see Countering Terrorism entry above), before the specialized anti-terrorism chamber rather than an ordinary correctional court. The case (the "affaire Ounissi") originates from his journalistic verification of information later used to summon him before the anti-terrorism unit as early as March 2024. A hearing was held July 10, 2026 without El Heni, his family, or his defense counsel being informed and without his transfer to court, drawing condemnation from his support committee and press-freedom advocates over the denial of basic defense-rights guarantees (businessnews.com.tn, July 10, 2026). No verdict has been reported as of this run. Update (Run 260): the case's namesake, Mondher Ounissi (Ennahdha's former interim party president), has his own parallel proceeding before the same specialized anti-terrorism chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance, over the online circulation of audio recordings attributed to him discussing a meeting with businessmen and alleged Ennahdha-linked financial violations — the underlying facts El Heni is accused of having journalistically verified. On July 11, 2026, the chamber rejected Ounissi's release request and postponed his case to September 25, 2026 (Kapitalis, Business News, July 11, 2026). No verdict has been reported for either defendant as of this run. Update (Run 338): journalist and radio chronicler Haythem El Mekki, previously cleared by a first-instance non-lieu (dismissal), had that dismissal reversed by the Sfax Court of Appeal on July 15, 2026, which convicted him and imposed a one-year prison sentence under this Article 86, over a January 2024 Facebook post criticizing internal conditions at Sfax's Habib Bourguiba University Hospital (whose administration filed the original complaint). Proceedings had initially been opened under Decree-Law No. 2022-54 before the conviction was ultimately entered under Article 86 instead — the same charge-substitution pattern already documented above for El Heni and Khiari. The SNJT (National Union of Tunisian Journalists) condemned the ruling and announced it would pursue a cassation appeal. Independently corroborated by Kapitalis, Business News, L'Économiste Maghrébin, webdo.tn, and Gnet News (all July 15, 2026). Update (Run 393): a distinct, older case became public July 17, 2026: journalist Hamza Belloumi and journalist Insaf Boughdiri (former editor-in-chief of the political program "Le Huitième Jour"/Al Hiwar Ettounsi) were convicted in absentia by the Tunis Court of Appeal in June 2026 to one year's imprisonment plus an 8,000-dinar fine each, in a prosecution dating to a May 2015 complaint by then-president Moncef Marzouki over a video segment aired on their program that was later shown to have been edited to falsely suggest he called for violence. The investigating judge at the Manouba tribunal had already rejected the plaintiff's "criminal conspiracy" (association de malfaiteurs) theory against the pair (and co-defendants Sofiène Ben Hamida and Noureddine Ben Ticha), characterizing the facts instead as professional misconduct without intent to defame; a Manouba first-instance default judgment in 2024 imposed only a fine, before the prosecution's appeal produced the harsher June 2026 custodial sentence, which the SNJT's July 18, 2026 statement links to the broader pattern of Article 86 prosecutions documented above for El Heni, Khiari, and El Mekki. Both defendants say they were never notified of any hearing at either instance and so could not exercise their right to defense; they have filed opposition, with a hearing before the Tunis Court of Appeal set for July 21, 2026 — outcome not yet known as of this run. Independently corroborated by Business News (three separate pieces), Kapitalis, Directinfo/WebManagerCenter, La Presse de Tunisie, Réalités Magazine, and allAfrica. Update (Run 463): that July 21, 2026 opposition hearing concluded — the Tunis Court of Appeal's correctional chamber accepted the opposition, annulled the in-absentia judgment against both journalists, and referred the case back for a full new examination on the merits, with the next hearing set for December 2, 2026. Corroborated by La Presse, Directinfo/WebManagerCenter, and Kapitalis (all July 21–22, 2026). Update (Run 517): a further case surfaced by the same SNJT July 18, 2026 statement, though on a materially different footing from the cases above: journalist and former HAICA council member Hichem Snoussi was convicted in absentia by the Tunis Court of First Instance to six months' imprisonment, in a case tied to a complaint filed years earlier by Nessma TV founder Nabil Karoui over statements Snoussi made — while serving on HAICA — about Nessma's ownership structure and the identity of its owners. Unlike the El Heni, Khiari, El Mekki, and Belloumi/Boughdiri cases above, no source found specifies the particular statute underlying the conviction (Article 86 is not independently confirmed for this case, only inferred from the pattern), so it is noted here as a related but not confirmed-identical charge. As with Belloumi/Boughdiri, Snoussi says he was not properly notified of the proceedings; he has since filed an opposition, with a new hearing set for October 2026. Sourced solely to Business News' July 18, 2026 report of Snoussi's own public statement — a second independent outlet was searched for but not found, so this is flagged as single-source pending future corroboration, consistent with this artifact's practice for time-sensitive same-day findings.
DigitalPress

Postal Code — Law No. 98-38 of June 2, 1998

Enacted: June 2, 1998 | Published: JORT N°44, June 2, 1998 | Amended: Law No. 2007-40
Sets the conditions for exercising postal activity in Tunisia and guarantees public access to basic postal services while protecting correspondence confidentiality. Governs licensing of postal operators — including the state operator, Office National des Postes (La Poste Tunisienne) — prohibited items, and sanctions for postal offenses. Unlike the telecommunications sector's independent regulator (INT), no standalone postal-sector regulatory authority has been identified; oversight remains with the Ministry of Communication Technologies and La Poste Tunisienne itself.
DigitalAdministrative

Draft "Code du Numérique" (Digital Code)Proposed Replacement of the 2001 Telecommunications Code

Ministerial draft publicly discussed 2025–2026 | Not yet submitted to the ARP as of July 2026
A comprehensive ~258-article draft code, prepared under the Ministry of Communication Technologies, intended to entirely replace the Telecommunications Code (Law No. 2001-1 of January 15, 2001). As publicly previewed by the Ministry, the draft would deregulate market entry for mobile virtual network operators (MVNOs), internet service providers, and data-center/cloud operators; impose "dig-once" shared-infrastructure obligations on network buildout; and restrict formal licensing requirements to genuinely scarce resources (radio spectrum, submarine-cable landing rights, satellite orbital slots), removing licensing for activities not dependent on such resources. It is also intended to consolidate cybersecurity, data-hosting, and digital-services rules currently scattered across the ICT Security Law, cybersecurity decree-laws, and sector-specific texts. The existing Cybercrime Law (Law No. 99-89) is expressly described by commentators as pre-dating this effort. As of July 2026 the draft remains a ministerial/preparatory text and has not been formally tabled before the Assembly of People's Representatives; the 2001 Telecommunications Code remains in force. Update (Run 66): within the existing (unamended) 2001 Code, the government nonetheless completed a full 5G rollout: a roadmap was approved and a licensing committee formed on June 13, 2024, licensing conventions were signed with Tunisie Telecom, Ooredoo, and Orange on November 30, 2024, and amending decrees granting each operator 5 MHz (700 MHz band) and 100 MHz (3.5 GHz band) for 15-year terms were published in JORT No. 8 of January 23, 2025; commercial 5G launched February 14, 2025.
Digital

Starlink / Satellite Internet Authorization (pending)

SpaceX request filed: January 2023 | Pilot test: Tunis, Ariana, Gabès (late 2023) | INT feasibility study: March–May 2025 | Status: unauthorized as of mid-2026 (Run 66 addition)
A wholly new standalone entry: Tunisia has neither authorized nor banned SpaceX's Starlink satellite-internet service, leaving a multi-year regulatory process stalled under the existing Telecommunications Code. Following SpaceX's January 2023 authorization request and a three-month pilot in Tunis, Ariana, and Gabès, the national regulator (INT — Instance Nationale des Télécommunications) issued a call for proposals in March 2025 for a feasibility study on satellite very-high-speed internet access, publishing results around May 5–6, 2025 reportedly recommending licensing of at least two LEO satellite operators under 5-year terms. As of early-to-mid 2026, Starlink's own coverage map lists Tunisia as "available from 2026" and takes refundable pre-order deposits, but no commercial license had been granted; the reported sticking point is INT's demand for local ground-station/national IP routing versus SpaceX's standard direct-to-gateway model. The long-pending "Code du Numérique" (see entry above) would eventually restrict licensing specifically to scarce resources including satellite orbital slots, potentially reshaping this process once enacted.
DigitalPending

Law No. 2004-5 of February 3, 2004 & Decree No. 2015-1032ICT Security and National Cybersecurity Agency

Law: February 3, 2004 | ANSI Decree: August 4, 2015
The 2004 ICT Security Law mandates security audits and certification for sensitive IT systems connected to public networks. Decree No. 2015-1032 created the National Cybersecurity Agency of Tunisia (ANSI — Agence Nationale de la Sécurité Informatique), responsible for cybersecurity policy, incident response (CERT-TN), and certification of secure IT systems for public entities and critical infrastructure operators.
DigitalSecurity

BCT Circular No. 2018-02 (January 25, 2018)Cryptocurrency Regulatory Ban

Issued: January 25, 2018 | Status: In force as of June 2026
The Central Bank of Tunisia explicitly banned all cryptocurrency transactions (purchase, holding, trading, mining, exchange) via Circular 2018-02. Violations subject to Exchange Control Law penalties. Despite a 2021 Finance Minister statement favoring decriminalization and a 2022 parliamentary proposal, no legislation has been enacted to legalize cryptocurrencies as of 2026. The BCT maintains a blockchain research sandbox (Digital Dinar experiments) separate from the ban.
DigitalFinancial

BCT Fintech Regulatory Sandbox — Circular No. 2020-03

Established: 2020 | Operative: 2020–present
The Central Bank of Tunisia established a regulatory sandbox for fintech innovations including mobile payments, digital wallets, peer-to-peer lending platforms, and open banking services. Applications reviewed by BCT's Innovation Committee. Sandbox participants may test services for up to 24 months before full licensing requirements apply. Part of BCT's Digital Finance Strategy 2025 targeting financial inclusion and payment modernization. (Run 188 addition): a concrete, well-corroborated licensing outcome under this framework: on February 18, 2026, Ooredoo Fintech Tunisie obtained a full BCT agrément (regulatory approval, distinct from mere sandbox admission) to launch "walletii by Ooredoo," a digital wallet offering bill payment, money transfer, and cash withdrawal, drawing on Ooredoo Fintech International's existing operations in Qatar, Oman, and the Maldives (La Presse de Tunisie, Business News, Managers.tn, Leaders.com.tn, and five further independent outlets, all February 17–18, 2026).
DigitalFinancial

Law No. 2000-83 of August 9, 2000Electronic Commerce and Digital Signatures

Enacted: August 9, 2000 | In force: 2000 | Amended: 2004
First statute to legally recognize electronic contracts and digital signatures in Tunisia. Establishes the legal equivalence of electronic documents and handwritten signatures. Governs online commercial transactions, electronic payment, and online offers. Establishes the legal framework for certification authorities issuing digital certificates. Foundation for Tunisia's e-commerce sector and e-government services.
DigitalCommercial

Proposition de loi n° 42/2024Regulation of Online Commerce & Social Media Marketing (Pending)

Deposited: May 2024, by 88 co-sponsoring deputies | ARP committee sessions through June 2026, examination ongoing | Status: not enacted as of July 1, 2026
A 49-article member's bill, deposited May 2024 by 88 co-sponsoring deputies, under review by the ARP's Committee on Administrative Organization, Development, Digitalization, Governance and Anti-Corruption, aimed at regulating commercial sales and marketing conducted through social media platforms (Facebook, TikTok, Instagram, etc.), a fast-growing informal segment of Tunisia's e-commerce sector. As proposed, it would require online sellers to obtain a "cahier des charges" (specifications file) from the Ministry of Commerce before operating, include a dedicated chapter on consumer-rights and personal-data protection (barring non-disclosure of a seller's identity, phone contacts, licenses, or commercial-registry details), and grant buyers a three-day right of withdrawal (droit de rétractation) to exchange or obtain a refund without justification. The Ministry of Commerce submitted written observations arguing that existing commercial law already partly covers online sales and separately announced its own e-commerce development strategy running through 2030, while the committee weighed whether to replace the mandatory cahier des charges with a simpler electronic declaration system. Correction: examination was not finalized on June 2, 2026 as previously tracked — reporting instead indicates the committee decided to form a dedicated working group at the end of an early-June session, suggesting unresolved points on the cahier-des-charges-vs-declaration debate remained open; the exact date of that session was not clearly confirmed in available sources. Update (Run 39): a June 24, 2026 WebManagerCenter piece characterized the working group's text as having reached a "near-final" shape, but this is a single secondary source rather than a confirmed committee vote or report, so it should be read as directional progress, not resolution of the underlying cahier-des-charges-vs-declaration question. Distinct from, and would supplement, the 2000 Electronic Commerce Law above, which does not address social-media-specific sales. Not yet adopted.
DigitalCommercial

Law No. 2000-57 of June 13, 2000Electronic Signatures and Certification Authorities

Enacted: June 13, 2000 | In force: 2000 | Complemented by: Decree No. 2000-2333
Establishes the legal framework for electronic signature services in Tunisia, predating the more general e-commerce law. Creates the Agence Nationale de Certification Électronique (ANCE — now part of the ANSI framework) as the root certification authority. Defines qualified electronic signatures, simple electronic signatures, and their respective legal values. Authorizes accredited certification service providers (PSC — prestataires de services de certification) to issue digital certificates. The implementing decree sets technical standards (PKI architecture, key lengths, certificate validity periods).
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–4: Definitions of electronic signature; equivalence with handwritten signature when qualified
  • Art. 5–9: Requirements for qualified electronic signatures (linked to signatory, capable of identifying signatory, created by controlled means)
  • Art. 10–14: Obligations of certification service providers; liability for certificate errors
  • Art. 15–18: Accreditation of PSCs by ANCE; ongoing audit requirements
  • Art. 19–22: Certificate suspension and revocation procedures
  • Art. 23–27: Penal sanctions for fraudulent use of certificates
DigitalCommercial

Decree-Law No. 2023-17 of March 11, 2023National Cybersecurity Framework & Cloud Computing Regulation

Enacted: March 11, 2023 | In force: March 2023 | Implementing decrees: 2024
Landmark post-2022 cybersecurity decree establishing a comprehensive national framework for cloud computing services and digital infrastructure security. As part of this decree, the National Agency for Computer Security (ANSI, created by Law No. 2004-5) was renamed the National Cybersecurity Agency (ANCS — Agence Nationale de la Cybersécurité). Mandates data localization requirements for "sensitive" public sector data (health, judicial, financial records must be stored on servers in Tunisia or in countries with adequate protection levels). Creates a licensing regime for cloud service providers operating for Tunisian public entities, including "secure" software/equipment labels and G-cloud/N-cloud labels for cloud providers. Strengthens the mandatory cybersecurity audit regime for critical infrastructure operators (energy, water, finance, telecom). Assigns ANCS enhanced enforcement powers including the ability to issue binding orders to address critical vulnerabilities. (Run 293 note): a separate, more comprehensive pending bill, Proposition de loi n° 2024-36, was under ARP committee examination as of early 2026 and would create a new National Cybersecurity Authority structure alongside this decree-law's existing ANCS framework — its relationship to (replacement of, or supplement to) this decree-law remains unclarified in available sources.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–6: Definitions — cloud computing, critical information infrastructure, sensitive data
  • Art. 7–14: Data localization obligations for public entities; list of sensitive data categories
  • Art. 15–22: Licensing of cloud service providers (IaaS, PaaS, SaaS) for public sector use
  • Art. 23–30: Mandatory security audits for critical infrastructure operators (minimum annual frequency)
  • Art. 31–38: ANSI enforcement powers; right to issue binding security directives
  • Art. 39–45: Incident reporting obligations (critical incidents: 4-hour notification to ANSI/CERT-TN)
  • Art. 46–52: Sanctions: up to 500,000 TND administrative fines + criminal penalties
DigitalSecurity

Organic Law No. 2024-22 of March 11, 2024Biometric National Identity Card

Adopted by ARP: March 6, 2024 (107–3–4) | Published: JORT, March 11, 2024 | Amends: Law No. 93-27 of March 22, 1993
Modifies and supplements the 1993 national identity card law to make a biometric, chip-based national ID card mandatory for citizens from age 15 (permitted with parental authorization from age 12). The chip embeds fingerprints, photograph, and encrypted personal data with access-logging safeguards; the reform also removes the profession field, adds a sex/gender data field, and eliminates the card's prior fixed expiration date. A companion statute of the same date, Organic Law No. 2024-23, separately establishes the biometric passport. Distinct from "e-Houwiya" (Mobile ID), an administrative digital-identity service launched by the Ministry of Communication Technologies in August 2022 (via Circulaire n°16 of August 3, 2022) that complements, rather than replaces, the physical biometric card created by this law.
DigitalAdministrative

Organic Law No. 2024-9 of February 6, 2024Accession to the Budapest Convention on Cybercrime

Enacted: February 6, 2024 | Instrument deposited: March 8, 2024
Approves Tunisia's accession to the Council of Europe Convention on Cybercrime (Budapest Convention, 2001), making Tunisia the 70th state party. Enables international cooperation on cybercrime investigation and prosecution, cross-border evidence-sharing, and extradition mechanisms, complementing the domestic cybercrime framework under Decree-Law No. 2022-54.
DigitalCriminal

Proposition de loi n° 2024-36Comprehensive Cybersecurity Framework (Pending)

Deposited: April 30, 2024 | ARP General Legislation Commission examination began: January 25, 2026 (minister hearings January 29, 2026) | Status: not enacted as of July 14, 2026
A member's bill (proposition de loi, as distinct from a government-initiated projet de loi) proposing a standalone, more comprehensive statutory cybersecurity framework than the existing Decree-Law No. 2023-17. As drafted, it would define "cybersecurity," "cyber threats," and "critical infrastructure"; require public entities and critical-infrastructure operators to adopt security policies and undergo regular audits; create a dedicated National Cybersecurity Authority together with a Strategic Cybersecurity Committee and a Crisis Management Committee (a more elaborate institutional structure than ANCS's existing mandate under Decree-Law No. 2023-17); impose security and incident-notification duties on telecom operators, ISPs, and digital service providers; mandate domestic hosting of sensitive data; and set administrative fines of 20,000–60,000 TND for violations (distinct from, and lower than, Decree-Law No. 2023-17's own 500,000 TND ceiling). The ARP's General Legislation Commission examined the bill starting January 25, 2026, hearing Communication Technologies Minister Nizar Ben Neji on January 29, 2026, alongside representatives of the Foreign Affairs and Interior ministries; no committee vote, floor debate, or JORT publication has been reported as of this run's cutoff, and its relationship to (replacement of, or supplement to) Decree-Law No. 2023-17 has not been clarified in any source reviewed. Also related to a separately reported, still-unconfirmed effort to reform Decree-Law No. 2022-54 on offenses involving information and communication systems. (Run 299 update): a direct WebFetch of legislation-securite.tn's own tracking page for this bill found its cross-reference field explicitly reads "Aucun texte n'est lié à ce texte" (no text is linked to this one) — meaning the tracking database itself records no formal linkage to Decree-Law No. 2023-17 or any other instrument. This clarifies, but does not fully resolve, the open question: the two texts appear to be tracked as legally independent rather than as amendment/replacement pairs, though this says nothing about whether the bill's own explanatory memorandum (not sighted) addresses the overlap in its stated legislative intent.
