Draft Laws (pending)State Guarantees for CPG (EBRD) & GCT (ITFC) Financing Agreements

Underlying EBRD guarantee agreement signed: November 3, 2025 | Underlying GCT/ITFC Mourabaha agreement signed: December 2, 2025 | ARP Bureau referral to Finance & Budget Committee: July 15, 2026 | GCT bill (n° 51/2026) under committee examination by emergency procedure: July 16, 2026 | Committee hearing on all three named bills: July 16–17, 2026 | Exceptional committee session held: Tuesday, July 21, 2026, 9:30 AM | Status: committee session concluded with a policy recommendation, not yet a bill vote
Following the same state-guarantee mechanism used for the STEG/IBRD loan guarantees above, the ARP Bureau referred a batch of five government bills to the Finance and Budget Committee on July 15, 2026, per African Manager's same-day report. Three of the five were named: (1) a bill approving a guarantee agreement dated November 3, 2025 between Tunisia and the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD) for a loan extended to the Compagnie des Phosphates de Gafsa (CPG) — Tunisia's principal public phosphate-mining entity, see the Mining Code entry — financing modernized mining equipment and a high-pressure wastewater filtration unit; and (2)–(3) two further bills approving Mourabaha (Islamic trade-finance) agreements with the International Islamic Trade Finance Corporation (ITFC) to finance fertilizer imports for the Groupe Chimique Tunisien (GCT), the state chemical/fertilizer group. The remaining two bills in the same referral batch were not named in available coverage. None of the five had reached a committee vote or plenary session as of this run. Not yet enacted. Update (Run 357): one of the two GCT/ITFC bills is now identified by number — bill n° 51/2026 — and, per a July 16, 2026 La Presse de Tunisie report, the Finance and Budget Committee began examining it under emergency procedure (procédure d'urgence) that day. The underlying Mourabaha agreement, signed December 2, 2025 between Tunisia and the ITFC, structures the $50 million guarantee as a revolving credit facility with multiple drawdowns, each repayable after 12 months, priced at SOFR + 3.75% plus a 0.4% arrangement fee, to fund GCT's import of raw materials for fertilizer production destined for both export and the domestic market. Still committee-stage; no vote or JORT publication reported as of this update. Update (Run 377): a July 17, 2026 La Presse de Tunisie report ("Phosphates: l'ARP examine trois financements et réclame une stratégie de réforme globale," independently corroborated by allAfrica's same-day republication) confirms the committee's July 16–17 session covered all three named bills together rather than only the n° 51/2026 GCT text Run 357 had isolated, and adds a substantive procedural detail beyond simple emergency-procedure examination: deputies withheld swift approval, instead demanding clarifications on how proceeds of CPG and GCT's previous loans (specifically financing meant for phosphate transport) had actually been used, and secured commitments for further hearings with the CPG and GCT chief executives plus representatives of the Ministries of Economy and Planning and of Industry, Mines and Energy before the committee proceeds further. The committee framed its role as extending beyond credit approval to rigorous monitoring of execution and real impact on public-enterprise governance, and called for a comprehensive phosphate-sector reform program alongside the financing — consistent with, and adding parliamentary-oversight detail to, the sector-wide reform pressure already documented at the Hydrocarbons Code and Mining Code entries' pending-reform notes. Still committee-stage; no vote, committee report, or JORT publication reported as of this update. Update (Run 423) — remaining two bills identified: per African Manager's July 15, 2026 report ("Le Bureau de l'ARP renvoie cinq projets de loi à la Commission des finances"), the five-bill referral batch is now fully named: bill (4) approves "avenant modificatif n°2" to the loan agreement between the Banque Centrale de Tunisie, acting on the state's behalf, and Afreximbank (the African Export-Import Bank — see the related Afreximbank US$500 Million Financing Facility entry above), amending the terms of part of that state-budget financing; and bill (5) approves the clôture du budget de l'État for fiscal year 2023 (formal closure of the FY2023 state budget accounts), an ordinary budget-execution formality distinct in kind from the CPG/GCT/Afreximbank financing-guarantee bills. Unlike bills (1)–(3), the Bureau's referral of bills (4)–(5) was not reported under the emergency (urgence) procedure. This closes the "two bills unidentified" caveat that had stood since Run 357/377; no vote or committee report has been reported for any of the five bills as of this update. Update (Run 434) — numbering completed, session confirmed, financing figure reconciled: a July 20, 2026 La Presse de Tunisie report ("ARP: examen de trois projets de loi pour financer la CPG et le Groupe chimique tunisien," independently corroborated the same day by allAfrica) confirms the Finance and Budget Committee will hold an exceptional session Tuesday, July 21, 2026 at 9:30 AM, with representatives of the Ministry of Industry, Mines and Energy and the Ministry of Economy and Planning in attendance, to examine all three named bills together. This closes the bill-numbering gap left open since Run 357: the CPG/EBRD guarantee is bill n° 49/2026, and the two GCT/ITFC Mourabaha guarantees are bills n° 50/2026 and n° 51/2026 (previously only n° 51/2026 had been individually numbered). The Presidency of the Republic itself requested the accelerated (urgence) examination procedure for all three, and the ARP Bureau has recommended consolidating bills n° 50/2026 and n° 51/2026 into a single committee report to speed plenary adoption. The article also reconciles the GCT financing figure: rather than a single $50 million facility (bill n° 51/2026 alone, per Run 357), the two GCT/ITFC Mourabaha agreements together constitute a renewable credit facility of up to $70 million — the earlier $50 million figure is not contradicted so much as superseded by this combined-facility total, which was not available until both GCT bills were identified. Still committee-stage as of this update; no vote or committee report has been reported for bills 49–51/2026. Update (Run 449) — exceptional session concluded, no bill vote yet: the Tuesday, July 21, 2026, 9:30 AM session confirmed at Run 434 was held as scheduled; per an African Manager report published the same day ("L'ARP exige le suivi des financements de la CPG et du GCT"), deputies used the session to press CPG and GCT representatives on the sector's underlying finances rather than proceeding straight to a vote on bills 49–51/2026, again criticizing the absence of a clear strategic vision and reform program for the phosphate sector (echoing the Run 377 findings) despite the CPG/GCT leadership's previously-documented 9.4-million-tonne 2035 production target and TND 2.7 billion investment plan. The committee closed the session by recommending exceptional, urgent measures to stabilize CPG and GCT's finances — enabling them to honor supplier commitments and restore confidence among banks and financing partners — alongside a "realistic and adequate" sector-revival strategy, rather than by voting the three guarantee bills themselves. No vote tally, committee report, or JORT publication for bills 49–51/2026 has been reported as of this update; the strategic-vision/reform-program demand remains the standing precondition the committee has now reiterated twice (Run 377 and Run 449) before it will advance the financing bills. Update (Run 521) — committee approval and plenary date set: per Réalités Magazine (July 23, 2026, "La commission des finances de l'ARP approuve trois projets de loi liés au financement du secteur des phosphates") and a same-day Managers.tn report, the Finance and Budget Committee moved from the Run 449 pressure session to an actual vote: bill n° 49/2026 (CPG/EBRD, €110 million) was approved by majority vote and bills n° 50–51/2026 (GCT/ITFC, $120 million combined) were adopted unanimously — cross-checked against La Presse de Tunisie and allAfrica's July 23, 2026 coverage citing a combined ~TND 750 million mobilized (110 million EBRD loan plus $120 million ITFC financing) for CPG/GCT stabilization. This is committee-level approval only, not a full ARP plenary vote — Managers.tn's own text explicitly frames implementation as still dependent on what follows. African Manager separately reports the ARP Bureau has now scheduled the plenary vote on all three bills for Tuesday, July 28, 2026, alongside a distinct, unrelated July 27, 2026 plenary session on nationwide water/electricity outages (an oversight session with no bill text, already tracked as a watch item on the Development Plan entry's changelog). No JORT publication has occurred; not yet enacted as of this update.
FinancialAdministrativePending

Law No. 2026-16 of July 20, 2026, Approving the 2026–2030 National Development Plan

Filed under Constitution Arts. 75 & 77 | ARP standing-committee examination: June 29 – July 2, 2026 (concluded) | Plenary session: July 9–10, 2026 | Adopted by ARP plenary: July 10, 2026 (64 for, 24 against, 15 abstentions) | Adopted by CNRD plenary: July 17, 2026 (56 for, 6 against, 5 abstentions) | Promulgated by President Kaïs Saïed: July 20, 2026 | Law No. 2026-16 of July 20, 2026, published JORT July 21, 2026 — confirmed, in force
The economic and social planning framework law for 2026–2030, formally deposited at the ARP as draft law n°42/2026 on June 17, 2026 following its adoption by the Council of Ministers. Structured as a single-article bill — the Assembly votes yes/no/abstain on the plan as a whole rather than amending individual provisions, per committee rapporteur Saber Jlassi — it was examined jointly by all Assembly of People's Representatives standing committees beginning June 29–30, 2026. Committee discussion noted the prior five-year plan's execution rate stood at under 40%, a benchmark cited by critical deputies alongside the new plan's more ambitious targets. Targets raising the average annual growth rate to 4.2% over the period (versus 2.4% in the prior five-year period), reaching 5.1% by 2030, and raising investment from roughly 15.5% to 20% of GDP via a public-investment project portfolio of approximately TND 102 billion. A June 30, 2026 joint hearing with Minister of Economy and Planning Samir Abdelhafidh preceded committee deliberations, followed by a second joint ARP audition of the Minister on July 1, 2026 to gather further committee input (Tunisian media noted the plan's limited treatment of the media/audiovisual sector as a specific point raised in that session, and a July 1, 2026 report noted several deputies voicing doubts about the feasibility of the plan's growth and investment targets, with the Finance Committee separately flagging concerns over the underlying economic and financial assumptions). Separately on July 1, 2026, the National Union of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT) publicly criticized the plan by name for omitting a dedicated media-sector strategy, calling for the assembly to formally recognize media as an economic and cultural sector, incorporate press-freedom legislation, and include the sector in the plan's monitoring mechanisms. The plan's headline targets include lowering the poverty rate to under 15%, a portfolio of roughly 21,100 public-investment projects funded at approximately TND 102 billion, and raising the renewable-energy share of electricity production to 35% by 2030; committee coverage also noted that of 40,748 development proposals submitted by local councils, 34,715 were retained in the plan's regional components. Once adopted, it is expected to serve as the legal reference framework underpinning the national AI strategy and the NDC 3.0 climate financing plan already noted elsewhere in this document. Update (Run 71): ARP President Ibrahim Bouderbala announced July 2, 2026 that the joint standing-committee examination had formally concluded — independently reported by both Le Temps News and African Manager (both July 3, 2026) — with a consolidated synthesis report now being drafted for submission to the ARP Bureau, which will schedule the plenary session before the text proceeds to the Conseil National des Régions et des Districts under Art. 77; no plenary date has yet been announced. Bouderbala used the same occasion to publicly call for accelerating adoption of the separately-tracked Foreign Exchange, Water, Hydrocarbons, and Investment codes (see those entries). Not yet enacted — the most active legislative process in Tunisia as of this update. Update (Run 141): Finance Commission member Issam Chouchane publicly criticized the plan on July 6, 2026 (Business News), arguing the document reads as a project list rather than a genuine strategic vision and lacks explicit objectives in several sectors he considers priorities, including food security, agriculture, phosphate, tourism, and new technologies/artificial intelligence; he also questioned the feasibility of the plan's average-growth target given recent economic performance, and reiterated that deputies hold only a consultative, up-or-down role on the single-article bill (echoing an earlier June 26, 2026 critique from the same deputy on the plan's financial feasibility). Update (Run 174): on Monday, July 6, 2026, the ARP Bureau formally referred the bill to a fresh round of joint examination by all permanent standing committees — chaired by the Assembly president or a vice-president — rather than sending it directly to plenary; a consolidated synthesis report from that joint round will go back to the Bureau, which will then schedule the plenary session (RTCI, July 6–7, 2026). No plenary date has yet been announced as of this update. Update (Run 185): the ARP Bureau met the morning of July 7, 2026 and formally scheduled the plenary session on the bill for Thursday, July 9, 2026, with the possibility of extending into Friday, July 10, 2026 if debate runs long (RTCI, July 7, 2026, 14:54, corroborated by a La Presse de Tunisie report of a 10:30 AM Thursday sitting) — the first concrete plenary date announced since the bill's June 17, 2026 filing. No vote outcome had been reported as of this update; still not yet enacted. Update (Run 202): the plenary session opened as scheduled at 10:30 AM on July 9, 2026 and floor debate ran the full day with no vote outcome reported by its close, consistent with the Bureau's built-in contingency to extend proceedings into July 10. Two deputies delivered the most substantive floor criticism of the bill documented to date. Deputy Mohamed Ali argued deputies were being asked to grant "un chèque en blanc pour cinq ans" (a blank check for five years) rather than vote on an ordinary law, since Parliament had not participated in drafting the plan and the single-article, up/down structure leaves no room for genuine amendment; he cited public debt of roughly TND 147 billion (over 80% of GDP) against a still-low national savings rate and a persistently deficient budget, faulted the plan for listing a large number of projects without a clear implementation priority ranking or demonstrated administrative capacity, and closed by calling on deputies to vote against the bill to defend "le droit du peuple à un développement réel, pas un développement sur papier" (Business News, July 9, 2026). Separately, ARP Bureau vice-president Nabil Hamdi — while confirming the Bureau's own procedural decision to send the bill to this plenary — criticized its substance, saying the plan in its current form "reflète davantage la volonté de l'administration que celle du peuple" (reflects the will of the administration more than that of the people), faulting the project portfolio as insufficiently detailed for the standing committees and citing what he called a "systematic exclusion" of Parliament from the plan's drafting phase (Radio Express FM, July 9, 2026). No JORT publication or confirmed floor vote had followed by this run's cutoff; still not yet enacted. Update (Run 218): the ARP plenary concluded debate on July 10, 2026 — Minister of Economy and Planning Samir Abdelhafidh replied to deputies' interventions before the vote (Tunisie Tribune, African Manager, same-day) — and adopted the bill in its entirety by a recorded vote of 64 for, 24 against, and 15 abstentions (La Presse de Tunisie, July 10, 2026), resolving the single most-tracked pending item across this changelog's recent runs. This vote outcome was, as of this update, reported only by La Presse among the outlets checked (African Manager and Tunisie Tribune's own July 10 pieces describe the session as still in progress ahead of the vote, likely filed before it concluded); flagged as single-source pending next-run corroboration, consistent with this artifact's established practice for time-sensitive same-day findings. The bill still requires presidential promulgation and JORT publication before entering into force — neither had been identified as of this update. Presidential promulgation and JORT publication remain outstanding as of this update; the companion STEG loan-guarantee bills (n°38/39-2026, see below) remain undecided despite reportedly sharing the same July 9–10 plenary window. Update (Run 232): a procedural detail not previously captured: per Constitution Art. 77's bicameral design, the plan bill does not proceed straight from ARP adoption to presidential promulgation — it must also pass a second-chamber vote at the Conseil National des Régions et des Districts (CNRD), with disagreements between the two chambers referred to a 10-member joint committee and, if still unresolved, final say reverting to the CNRD (Tunisie Numérique, quoting deputy Jmaï Zouidi). This clarifies why promulgation/JORT publication remain outstanding well past the July 10 ARP vote: a CNRD vote is a still-outstanding procedural step, not merely an administrative delay in signature. No CNRD vote date had been reported as of this update. Still no CNRD floor vote, presidential promulgation, or JORT publication confirmed as of this update. No CNRD floor vote, presidential promulgation, or JORT publication had been reported as of this update. 🌾 Agriculture & Fisheries

Fisheries Code — Law No. 94-13 of January 31, 1994 (as amended 2002, 2014)

Enacted: January 31, 1994 | In force: 1994 | Amended: 2002, 2014
Comprehensive statute governing fishing activities in Tunisian territorial waters and the EEZ. Commercial fishing vessels require licenses from the Ministry of Agriculture. Sets fishing quotas, prohibited fishing methods (dynamite, poison, trawling within 3 nm of coast), protected marine species, and seasonal closures. Establishes the National Fisheries Office (ONP — Office National des Pêches) for market organization and port management. Tuna and swordfish fishing regulated under ICCAT commitments. Aquaculture (fish farming) licensing governed by supplementary 2006 decree.
AgricultureEnvironmental

Plant Protection Law — Law No. 92-72 of August 3, 1992; Veterinary Law — Law No. 2005-35 of May 25, 2005

Plant Protection: August 3, 1992 | Veterinary: May 25, 2005
(v4.178 correction, Run 178): The plant-protection half of this entry previously cited "Law No. 99-24 of February 23, 1999" and an administering "INAP — National Institute for Plant Protection." Neither checks out: Law No. 99-24 is in fact dated March 9, 1999 and governs an unrelated subject — veterinary sanitary control at import and export — not plant health (confirmed via two independent legal-database sources), and no institute named "INAP" could be independently corroborated as Tunisia's plant-protection body (the confirmed body is the Direction Générale de la Santé Végétale et du Contrôle des Intrants Agricoles, under the Ministry of Agriculture). The correct citation for Tunisia's phytosanitary/plant-protection framework is Law No. 92-72 of August 3, 1992 (recasting prior phytosanitary legislation into a unified regime), independently confirmed via FAOLEX's full text and a second corroborating source. It prohibits import, possession, cultivation, and transit of quarantine pests absent authorization and conditions plant/plant-product imports on a phytosanitary certificate per the International Plant Protection Convention model (implementing detail set by ministerial order, e.g. a February 19, 2016 decree on import requirements and inspection procedures), with narrow exemptions for processed agricultural goods. The separate Law No. 99-24 of March 9, 1999 (veterinary sanitary control at import/export) remains a genuine, distinct Tunisian statute not otherwise documented in this artifact. The Veterinary Law (2005-35) citation is unchanged from prior runs and was not re-verified this pass; it regulates animal health surveillance, veterinary practice licensing, slaughterhouse standards, and control of notifiable animal diseases. Both plant- and animal-health regimes implement Tunisia's WTO Agreement on Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures (SPS Agreement) obligations for agricultural trade.
AgricultureHealth

Veterinary Pharmacy Law — Law No. 78-23 of March 8, 1978 (as amended by Law No. 2000-40 of April 5, 2000)Réglementation de la Pharmacie Vétérinaire (Run 174 addition)

Enacted: March 8, 1978 | Amended: April 5, 2000 | Implementing decree: Décret n° 79-831 of September 28, 1979
A previously undocumented, narrowly distinct statute from the Veterinary Law entry above: while Law No. 2005-35 governs animal health surveillance and veterinary practice licensing, this earlier 1978 law (as substantially amended in 2000) specifically regulates veterinary medicines — manufacturing-facility authorization, market-authorization (visa) applications and renewals for veterinary pharmaceutical products, and pharmacovigilance, administered by the Ministry of Public Health's Direction de la Pharmacie et du Médicament (DPM). Décret n° 79-831 of September 28, 1979 sets good-manufacturing-practice and quality-control standards, plus packaging/labeling rules, for veterinary-medicine production. This narrow medicines-licensing gap survived at least four prior targeted "veterinary" gap-scans (Runs 82, 90, 113, 116) that confirmed only the broader animal-health/practice statute, without distinguishing this separate pharmaceutical-regulation track — parallel to how Run 147 found the companion-animal-welfare gap after similar prior scans of adjacent terrain.
AgricultureHealth

Agricultural Development Law — Law No. 71-72 of December 31, 1971 and Law No. 2000-30 of March 20, 2000

Development Areas: 1971 | Irrigated Perimeters: March 20, 2000
The 1971 law empowers the Ministry of Agriculture to designate agricultural development areas with state support for irrigation and land improvement. The 2000 law governs management of public irrigated perimeters (périmètres irrigués publics — PIPs): water pricing through GDA user associations (Groupements de Développement Agricole), infrastructure maintenance, and cost recovery. Emergency water restrictions (2023 drought) were issued under the Water Code's provisions with inter-ministerial coordination.
AgricultureLand

Organic Agriculture Law — Law No. 99-30 of April 5, 1999

Enacted: April 5, 1999 | In force: 1999 | Amended: 2009
Establishes the legal framework for organic farming, certification, labeling, and inspection in Tunisia. Organic products must be certified by an accredited certification body and labeled with the national organic mark. Controls use of synthetic pesticides, fertilizers, and GMOs on certified organic land. Administered by the Ministry of Agriculture and the Office for Organic Farming Development (BIO). Tunisia exports significant volumes of certified organic olive oil and dates to EU markets.
AgricultureCommercial

Law No. 67-41 of June 24, 1967 (as amended)Agricultural Cooperatives

Enacted: June 24, 1967 | In force: 1967 | Amended: 1982, 2001, 2020
Establishes the legal framework for agricultural cooperatives (coopératives agricoles) in Tunisia. Agricultural cooperatives are governed by a hybrid regime combining principles from general cooperative law and specific agricultural provisions. Types include: production cooperatives, service cooperatives (input supply, marketing), and credit cooperatives (Caisses de Crédit Agricole Mutuels). Registration with the Ministry of Agriculture and the Office of Agricultural Credit Cooperatives (ODCO) is required. Cooperatives benefit from tax exemptions on profits and reduced registration fees. The Tunisian Cooperative Union (UTAP — Union Tunisienne de l'Agriculture et de la Pêche) represents cooperatives at national level. The 2020 amendment strengthened governance requirements and introduced digital voting for cooperative assemblies.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–10: Definition of agricultural cooperative; democratic governance (one member, one vote)
  • Art. 11–20: Types: production, marketing, supply, credit cooperatives
  • Art. 21–28: Registration requirements; statutes; minimum capital
  • Art. 29–36: Distribution of surplus to members proportional to participation
  • Art. 37–44: Tax advantages: exemption from corporate tax; reduced stamp duties
  • Art. 45–52: Dissolution and liquidation procedures
AgricultureCommercial

Law No. 67-4 of January 19, 1967 (Run 382 addition)General Statute of Cooperation (Statut Général de la Coopération)

Enacted: January 19, 1967 | In force: 1967
The base, cross-sectoral statute establishing the legal form and general principles of the cooperative society (société coopérative) in Tunisia — democratic one-member-one-vote governance, member-proportional surplus distribution, and a registration/dissolution regime — beneath which sector-specific texts operate, most prominently the Agricultural Cooperatives Law (No. 67-41, above) as well as separate consumer, housing, craft/industrial, and fishing cooperative regimes. Sourced via ILO NATLEX's Tunisia legislative database entry ("Loi n° 67-4 portant statut général de la coopération") and independently corroborated by the Coops4Dev/International Cooperative Alliance's 2020 legal-framework analysis of Tunisia, which describes Tunisian cooperative law as split across six category-specific texts sitting on this general 1967 base and — consistent with this artifact's practice of flagging rather than resolving genuine primary-source gaps — itself calls the resulting framework "overly complex," recommending eventual consolidation into a single modern code. This closes a gap first flagged as a low-confidence "genuinely ambiguous" open item at Run 129 and left unresolved across the roughly 250 runs since: the general statute's own text (as opposed to the agricultural-sector implementation already documented) had not previously been independently sourced. The full article-by-article text was not directly retrievable this run (NATLEX's detail page returned HTTP 403 and a related PDF was unparseable), so no line-item provisions are cited here pending a future run's direct-text confirmation; the law's existence, number, and general subject are corroborated by two independent sources.
AgricultureCommercial

Law No. 2001-94 of August 7, 2001Pastoral and Communal Lands Code

Enacted: August 7, 2001 | In force: 2001
Regulates the use and management of collective pastoral lands (terres collectives de parcours) and communal grazing areas in Tunisia, particularly relevant in the arid and semi-arid regions of central and southern Tunisia. These lands — covering approximately 5 million hectares — are traditionally managed by tribal communities. The code establishes a management framework balancing customary pastoral rights with sustainable land management obligations. Creates Pastoral Development Associations (associations de développement pastoral) for each defined rangeland unit. Prohibits unauthorized subdivision, sale, or permanent agricultural conversion of collective pastoral lands without Ministry of Agriculture approval. Provides mechanisms for resolving boundary disputes between pastoral communities.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–8: Definition of collective pastoral lands; state ownership with usufruct rights to communities
  • Art. 9–16: Pastoral Development Associations (PDA) — formation, governance, mandate
  • Art. 17–24: Seasonal grazing circuits; carrying capacity limits per zone
  • Art. 25–30: Prohibition of unauthorized permanent crop installation; penalties
  • Art. 31–36: Water point management in pastoral zones
  • Art. 37–42: Dispute resolution between communities; role of Ministry of Agriculture
AgricultureLand

Law No. 2019-25 of February 26, 2019Food and Animal Feed Safety

Enacted: February 26, 2019 | In force: 2019
(v4.178 correction, Run 178): This entry previously cited "Law No. 2009-11 of February 11, 2009" as the food-safety framework law and "ANCSEP" as the body it created — both incorrect. Law No. 2009-11 (March 2, 2009) is in fact Tunisia's building fire/explosion/panic-safety code, an entirely unrelated statute (confirmed via legislation-securite.tn and citet.nat.tn), and ANCSEP (Agence Nationale de Contrôle Sanitaire et Environnemental des Produits) was created earlier, by Decree No. 99-779 of April 5, 1999, as a general sanitary/environmental product-control coordinator — not a food-safety-specific body created by this law. The actual food-safety framework statute is Law No. 2019-25 of February 26, 2019, relative à la sécurité sanitaire des denrées alimentaires et des aliments pour animaux, which establishes a unified legal regime for foodstuff and animal-feed safety grounded in three principles: risk analysis based on scientific evidence, precaution, and transparency (public consultation and consumer information). It creates the National Authority for Food Safety (Instance Nationale de la Sécurité Sanitaire des Produits Alimentaires — INSSPA), which brings together representatives from the health, agriculture, and commerce ministries and functions as Tunisia's sole risk-management and risk-notification authority for food products — distinct from and complementing ANCSEP's broader coordination role. Mandates food-chain traceability from producer to consumer. Confirmed via FAOLEX's full text of the law and INSSPA/ANCSEP institutional-comparison sources; specific article numbers are not asserted here pending direct verification against the official JORT text, consistent with this project's practice of omitting unverifiable citations.
AgricultureHealthCommercial

Law No. 2026-5 of March 11, 2026Regularization of Distressed Agricultural Debts

Adopted by ARP: March 10, 2026 | Promulgated: March 11, 2026 | Provisions apply through: December 31, 2026
Requires public and private banks to reschedule the full principal and initial contractual interest of classified/distressed agricultural debts (BCT class 4 and above, held before September 30, 2025) owed by farmers and agricultural companies, over up to 7 years including a 1-year grace period. Mandates automatic removal of the resulting negative banking classification once a rescheduling agreement is approved, restoring debtors' access to new financing. The Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT) was required to issue implementing conditions within 15 days of publication. Excludes debts connected to unresolved corruption or money-laundering proceedings. Enacted to address accumulated financial distress in the agricultural sector that had left many farmers and agri-businesses unable to settle liabilities or obtain new credit.
AgricultureFinancial

Proposition de loi n° 74/2024 (pending)Animal Protection and Domestic-Carnivore Regulation

Filed: July 23, 2024 (Deputy Chokri Bahri) | Examined: ARP Agriculture, Food Security & Maritime Fishing Commission, May 5, 2026 | Status: not yet enacted as of July 2026
A member's bill that would modernize Tunisia's animal-protection framework — described by its authors as dating to 19th-century legislative instruments never adapted to current social, economic, and environmental conditions — and specifically regulate the detention and circulation of domestic carnivores (dogs and cats) amid a persistent stray-dog and rabies-control problem in Tunisian cities. Proposes shifting municipal stray-animal management away from mass culling campaigns toward prevention, registration, and sterilization programs, while strengthening penalties for cruelty and negligence. At the May 5, 2026 Agriculture Commission session, deputies broadly welcomed the initiative as aligning Tunisia with international animal-welfare standards but raised concerns about the technical and financial capacity of municipalities to run effective sterilization programs at scale. Remains at the committee-examination stage; no committee vote or plenary referral confirmed as of this run. (Added Run 147 — a wholly new standalone entry outside the pending-bill watchlist, closing a gap in the Agriculture & Fisheries section that prior runs' "veterinary/animal health" gap-scans had not distinguished from this separate animal-welfare/companion-animal track.) Update (Run 176): Review of the bill accelerated after a video showing a performance-troupe member dancing on the belly of a horse lying on the ground during a wedding ceremony in Sahline (Monastir governorate) went viral around July 6–7, 2026; the animal-protection association Arrahma announced it was pursuing a formal criminal complaint against the identified troupe (a second, initially accused troupe was cleared after verification) and separately called on Parliament to expedite the reform. Business News (July 7, 2026) reported the ARP was accelerating examination of the animal-welfare framework, and press coverage detailed proposed tiered penalties not previously captured here: 15 days'–1 month imprisonment plus a TND 500 fine for general mistreatment, rising to 1–10 years plus a TND 2,000 fine where abuse causes permanent disability, and 5 years where it causes death, with enhanced sentences for repeat offenders. Corroborated across Business News, Univers News, and allAfrica (citing Arrahma's president Jamila Ammar); no committee vote or plenary referral confirmed as of this run. This is the same Proposition de loi n° 74/2024 tracked above, not a separate bill, despite one headline's reference to "deux propositions de loi." Correction & update (Run 203): that Run 176 dismissal appears to have been premature. A separate Business News article with the same "deux propositions de loi" headline (July 7, 2026, sourcing deputy Mohamed Zied El Maher's same-day Diwan FM interview) was independently re-checked and, across three separately-worded searches, consistently identified Proposition de loi n° 66/2024 — also filed July 23, 2024 "by a group of deputies," also before the Agriculture Commission, which "has already heard the project initiators and several sector stakeholders" — as the bill carrying the reinforced financial/criminal sanctions for mistreatment, capture, or killing of an animal (i.e., the tiered-penalty provisions Run 176 attributed to n° 74/2024 above may in fact belong to this companion bill), while n° 74/2024 is described in this account as the complementary prevention/awareness text providing for animal-welfare and environmental-protection content in school curricula. This conflicts with the July 26, 2024 Kapitalis filing announcement (sourced directly via WebFetch), which names only Deputy Chokri Bahri and a single unnumbered bill combining both sanction-strengthening and "culture of respect" language — the article that appears to underlie this artifact's existing n° 74/2024 attribution above. Given this unresolved conflict between a 2024 primary filing source (one bill, one sponsor, mixed content) and 2026 committee-status coverage (two numbered bills, split by content) — and a failed direct WebFetch of the July 7, 2026 Business News article itself (HTTP 503) that prevented full-text confirmation — this update stops short of re-splitting the entry or reattributing content, and instead flags Proposition de loi n° 66/2024 as a probable second, separately-numbered animal-protection bill before the same Agriculture Commission, to be confirmed or resolved by a future run with direct access to the ARP's bill registry or the source article's full text. Update (Run 204): a direct WebFetch of the same July 7, 2026 Business News article again failed (HTTP 503, matching Run 203's failure and confirming this is a persistent access issue rather than a transient one), so full-text confirmation still could not be obtained; however, two independently-worded WebSearch queries run this pass both returned AI-generated summaries of that article converging on the same account: Proposition de loi n° 66/2024 (filed July 23, 2024 "by a group of deputies") is the bill carrying the reinforced sanctions — a summary this run renders as 1–3 years' imprisonment plus a fine of up to TND 2,000 where abuse causes permanent injury, rising to 5 years where it causes death (qualified as a "crime" rather than a lesser offense) — while n° 74/2024 remains the complementary prevention/awareness bill (school-curriculum content on animal welfare and environmental protection). The same summaries quote deputy Mohamed Zied El Maher explaining the delay since 2024: government observations prompted reworking of the text's legal definitions and implementation modalities, and the Agriculture Commission has already heard both the bills' initiators and sector stakeholders. Note the sanction figures in this account (1–3 years/TND 2,000/permanent injury; 5 years/death) do not exactly match the tiered penalties Run 176 sourced to the same underlying reporting (15 days'–1 month/TND 500/general mistreatment; 1–10 years/TND 2,000/permanent disability; 5 years/death) — since both derive from search-engine summarization rather than a directly fetched primary text, this discrepancy is flagged rather than silently resolved in either direction. On balance this run treats the bill-number split (n° 66/2024 = sanctions, n° 74/2024 = prevention) as now reasonably well-corroborated across three independent search passes (Runs 203 and 204), even though the exact sanction figures remain unconfirmed pending a successful direct fetch or ARP registry check. Update (Run 423): a Business News article dated May 6, 2026 (the day after the already-cited May 5, 2026 Agriculture Commission session) describes the bill under review that day as a single comprehensive structuring text of 12 titles and 111 articles, built around a "one health" (santé unique, i.e. human/animal/environmental health interdependence) framework, that would also mandate minimum living-condition standards for kept animals and introduce electronic identification/microchipping for domestic carnivores, alongside the sterilization-vs-culling debate already documented above. This 12-title/111-article structure does not obviously map onto either the "n° 66/2024" or "n° 74/2024" bill numbers already flagged as unresolved above, and may describe a consolidated version of the text presented to that same session rather than a third bill — flagged here as an additional, not-yet-reconciled data point rather than a resolution of the existing numbering ambiguity.
AgriculturePending

🏛️ Tourism & Cultural Heritage

Tourism Organization Law — Law No. 2012-49 of July 2, 2012 (Code du Tourisme)

Enacted: July 2, 2012 | In force: 2012 | Replaced: Decree-Laws No. 73-3 & 73-4 of October 3, 1973 (ratified by Laws No. 73-58 & 73-59 of November 19, 1973) and their implementing classification Decree No. 73-510 of October 30, 1973
Governs all tourism activities in Tunisia: classification and licensing of hotels (1–5 stars), travel agencies, tour operators, holiday villages, and tourist guides. Hotels classified by the Ministry of Tourism with annual inspection. The National Tourism Office (ONTT — Office National du Tourisme Tunisien) promotes tourism internationally. Investment incentives for hotel renovation under the Investment Law 2016. Tourism zone master plans govern coastal development. Replaced the sector's original 1973 framework — Decree-Law 73-3 (ratified by Law 73-58) controlled tourist-establishment management/operation, Decree-Law 73-4 (ratified by Law 73-59) controlled their construction, and Decree No. 73-510 set hotel classification standards (itself amended in 1983 and 1986, later updated by Decree No. 2007-457 of March 6, 2007, prior to this 2012 Code's full recodification). 2025: Draft Law No. 93/2025 proposed 30% social tourism quota, generating hotel federation opposition. (Run 174 addition): a second, previously undocumented reform bill was examined alongside the social-tourism quota at the same ARP Tourism Committee session on March 11, 2026 — a proposal to replace the tourist-guide licensing regime, unchanged since the 1970s, with stricter qualification requirements (specialized training, Tunisian history/culture knowledge, foreign-language proficiency); as of that session, the committee had not yet decided whether to amend the existing framework or draft an entirely new one, and said it would consult further stakeholders before finalizing the text (La Presse, Managers.tn, both March 2026). No later committee action or vote has been found as of this run.
TourismCommercial

Law No. 2008-33 of May 13, 2008Shared-Time Tourist Accommodation (Hébergement Touristique à Temps Partagé)

Enacted: May 13, 2008 | Published: JORT No. 40, May 16, 2008 | Implementing texts: Decree No. 2009-1935 of June 15, 2009; Ministerial Order of June 16, 2009
Regulates Tunisia's "timeshare" tourist-accommodation sector, distinct from the general Tourism Organization Law above. Amends an earlier 1997 framework and requires operators to obtain prior authorization before offering shared-time accommodation rights. Characterizes the right acquired by a purchaser as a droit de jouissance (usufruct-type real right) rather than co-ownership (multipropriété) as sometimes marketed — a deliberate divergence from the ownership model used in some other jurisdictions. Decree No. 2009-1935 (June 15, 2009) sets the conditions for obtaining the required prior operating authorization; a companion Ministerial Order of the same month approved a mandatory standard contract requiring at least 13 provisions (parties' identities, subject matter, duration, price, statutory reflection/cooling-off period, management arrangements, termination modalities, and dispute resolution). JurisiteTunisie's consumer guidance separately warns prospective buyers about timeshare-marketing scams predating and motivating the law. Never previously documented in 132 prior research runs.
TourismCivilCommercial

Cultural Heritage Protection Law — Law No. 94-35 of February 24, 1994

Enacted: February 24, 1994 | In force: 1994 | Amended: 2013
Classifies and protects Tunisia's movable and immovable cultural heritage: archaeological sites, historic monuments (medinas, mosques, ribats, Roman amphitheaters), traditional crafts, and museum collections. Unauthorized excavation, construction near classified sites, and export of antiquities are criminal offenses. The National Heritage Institute (INP — Institut National du Patrimoine) manages classified sites. Tunisia has 8 UNESCO World Heritage Sites (Carthage, Medina of Tunis, El Jem Amphitheatre, Dougga, Ichkeul National Park, Kerkouane, Kairouan, Sousse Medina). Implements the 1972 UNESCO World Heritage Convention (ratified 1975).
Key provisions
  • Classification of monuments, sites, and historic districts by ministerial order
  • Protection zones (périmètres de sauvegarde) around classified sites
  • Prohibition on unauthorized excavation — criminal penalties
  • Export of classified antiquities prohibited; unlawful export criminalized
  • INP manages museums and archaeological parks
  • International cooperation with UNESCO, ICOMOS, and bilateral partners
TourismEnvironmental

Crafts Law — Law No. 2009-25 of May 23, 2009 (Loi relative à l'artisanat)

Enacted: May 23, 2009 | In force: 2009
Defines and protects traditional Tunisian crafts (artisanat). Establishes the National Office of Tunisian Crafts (ONAT — Office National de l'Artisanat Tunisien). Creates the "artisan" professional status with access to specific microfinancing (BTS loans). Protects geographical craft designations (e.g., "Tapis de Kairouan," "Poterie de Nabeul," "Chéchia de Tunis"). Quality certification and export promotion administered by ONAT. The crafts sector employs over 300,000 artisans and is a significant contributor to tourism revenue.
TourismCommercial

Cinema and Cultural Industries — Law No. 93-40 of April 26, 1993 and Ministerial Orders

Cinema framework: April 26, 1993 | Ongoing regulatory updates | Reform bill: Proposition de loi n° 2024-03, committee review ongoing as of June 2026
Governs Tunisian cinema production, distribution, and exhibition. Establishes the Centre National du Cinéma et de l'Image (CNCI) which administers production subsidies, co-production agreements, and film classification. Tunisia maintains an active film industry with international co-productions. The broader cultural sector is governed by the Ministry of Cultural Affairs, which funds the national theater (TNT), opera (OCT), symphonic orchestra, and public libraries network. Cultural associations are registered under Decree 88 of 2011. Pending reform: Proposition de loi n° 2024-03, a dedicated statute to organize, structure, and develop the cinema industry, has been under review by the ARP's Tourism, Culture, Services, and Handicrafts Committee. Correction: the June 24, 2026 session was described in committee reporting as "continuing the study" (mouwasalat dirasa) of the bill rather than a completed article-by-article finalization as previously tracked; no committee report has been confirmed as finalized. Not yet referred to plenary as of this run. Update (Run 39): independent of this pending reform, the sector's existing subsidy mechanism was reinforced through ordinary budget legislation — Article 61 of the Finance Law 2026 (Law No. 2025-17, see Tax & Fiscal section) earmarks approximately TND 100 million per year for the CNCI's production-support fund, a concrete funding increase that took effect January 1, 2026 ahead of, and separate from, the still-pending organizational reform bill above. Update (Run 137): secondary reporting on the June 24, 2026 committee session (already cited above) additionally describes the bill as introducing an age-based film-classification system in place of the CNCI's current prior-censorship-style review, and states it would consolidate or replace older, narrower cinema-sector texts alongside the CNCI's own founding framework. This classification detail is plausible and consistent with the bill's stated purpose, but the specific predecessor-statute numbers given in that reporting could not be independently reconciled with this entry's verified Law No. 93-40/CNCI citation, so they are deliberately omitted pending a primary ARP/JORT text — treat as an open sourcing question for a future run rather than a correction. Update (Run 171): a further, distinct fiscal measure for the sector — Proposition de loi n° 103, adopted by ARP floor vote on December 3, 2025 (57 for, 34 against, 8 abstentions) — sets a single 7% tax rate on equipment for cinematographic, photographic, and audiovisual production by legally-declared enterprises, plus a full (0%) customs-duty exemption on imports of such equipment; corroborated via two independent sources (La Presse, Dec. 3, 2025; WebManagerCenter/Directinfo, Dec. 3, 2025). The Finance Minister flagged the measure with reservations during the floor debate, noting the sector already receives fiscal advantages, and described the article as regulatory text requiring separate issuance rather than an amendment folded directly into the Finance Law 2026 — no JORT publication confirming its issuance has been identified as of this run, so its current in-force status should be treated as unresolved rather than assumed enacted.
TourismPress

Law No. 2026-8 of May 13, 2026Status of the Artist and Artistic Professions

Adopted by the ARP and published in JORT: May 13, 2026 | In force: 2026
New law defining and regulating the legal status of artists and artistic professions, adopted after more than a decade of debate and lobbying by cultural-sector unions — entirely absent from this artifact despite 39 prior research runs and the existing cinema-industry entry above covering the adjacent film-subsidy framework. Establishes formal recognition and professional-card registration for artists across disciplines, and notably extends recognition to digital-arts practitioners and questions of AI-assisted creative work — one of the only Tunisian statutes to date touching generative-AI authorship issues, albeit narrowly, within the cultural sector rather than as general AI regulation (compare the still-pending Personal Data Protection & AI bill in the Digital & ICT section, which remains the primary vehicle under consideration for broader AI governance). Update (Run 423): the ARP plenary adopted the bill 76 for, 1 against, 0 abstentions on May 12, 2026, one day ahead of the May 13, 2026 JORT publication date already cited above; the enacted text runs to 45 articles and, beyond the professional-card mechanism, sets a minimum-quota requirement for Tunisian artistic content on national broadcasters, a detail not previously captured here.
AdministrativeLaborTourism

Law No. 88-95 of August 2, 1988 (as amended)National Archives, Libraries, and Documentary Heritage

Enacted: August 2, 1988 | In force: 1989 | Amended: 2002, 2018
Establishes the legal framework for the Tunisian national archives, public libraries, and documentary heritage preservation. Creates the National Archives of Tunisia (Archives Nationales de Tunisie — ANT) as the authority responsible for collecting, preserving, and providing access to historical state archives. Mandates transfer of public documents from ministries and agencies to the ANT after statutory retention periods (typically 30 years for classified documents). Establishes the National Library of Tunisia (Bibliothèque Nationale de Tunisie — BNT), receiving legal deposit copies of all publications printed in Tunisia (2 copies of each work). Governs public library network (bibliothèques de lecture publique) in each governorate. The 2018 amendment introduced digital archiving standards and authorized digitization programs for rare manuscripts, especially the Zeitouna manuscript collection (one of the largest Islamic manuscript collections in North Africa).
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–8: National Archives mandate; ANT organizational structure under Ministry of Culture
  • Art. 9–16: Document retention schedules; mandatory transfer to ANT after retention period
  • Art. 17–22: Public access to archives: 30-year rule for ordinary documents; 60-year rule for sensitive documents; 100-year rule for personal data
  • Art. 23–28: Legal deposit obligation for all Tunisian publishers (books, periodicals, maps, audiovisual)
  • Art. 29–34: National Library structure; special collections (Arabo-Islamic manuscripts)
  • Art. 35–40: Public library network; municipal libraries under regional cultural authorities
  • 2018 amendment: Digital preservation strategy; mass digitization of fragile and rare items
TourismEducation

Tourism Investment Code — Law No. 90-21 of March 19, 1990Code des Investissements Touristiques (Run 172 addition)

Enacted: March 19, 1990 | In force: 1990 | Partially superseded: Investment Law No. 2016-71 (see Investment & Business section)
A wholly new standalone entry — a genuinely uncovered sector-specific investment statute, distinct from the general Tourism Organization Law (2012-49, licensing/classification) and the general Investment Law (2016-71, see Investment & Business section). While the 2016 law repealed the general 1993/1994 incentive codes it replaced, several tourism-specific articles of the 1990 code — Arts. 3, 5, 6, 7, and 8, covering state land allocation to hotel/tourist-residence projects, sector-specific project-approval procedures, and tourism-project financing conditions — remain explicitly in force alongside it, since the 2016 code did not expressly repeal them. One of the tracked pending bills in this section's Tourism Organization Law entry (Draft Law No. 93/2025's social-tourism quota) operates within this same sector-specific-incentive space rather than superseding it.
TourismFinancial

🏆 Sport & Youth

Sport Law — Organic Law No. 95-11 of February 6, 1995(as amended) (Run 44 correction)

Original: February 6, 1995 | Amended: reportedly 2004 and 2006 (grew from 24 to 28 articles)
Run 44 correction: prior runs cited an amendment by "Law No. 2014-17 of April 14, 2014" — no such law could be verified in any source searched, and it appears to have been a fabricated citation; drop it rather than repeat it. Multiple independent secondary sources (a university thesis on Tunisian sports associations, kapitalis.com) instead describe the law as "modified and supplemented on 3 occasions, once in 2004 and a second time in 2006," growing from 24 to 28 articles — the exact JORT numbers for these amendments could not be confirmed from available sources and should be verified against a primary text before being cited as fact. Governs the organization of sport in Tunisia: national sports federations (a federation groups specialized associations within one or more disciplines), sports clubs, ministerial model-statutes and dissolution power over mismanaged federation boards, minimum 20% of public subsidies earmarked for youth training, and the amateur/non-amateur (professional) practice framework. Complemented by Law No. 94-104 of August 3, 1994 (physical-education and sports-activity organization: national sports council, Comité Olympique Tunisien, school/university and disabled-sports federations), which itself replaced Law No. 84-63 of 1984. Tunisia adheres to the World Anti-Doping Code; the Tunisian Anti-Doping Agency (LTDLA) is the national anti-doping authority.
SportAdministrative

Youth Policy — Law No. 2002-51 of May 13, 2002 and National Youth Strategy 2022

Framework law: May 13, 2002 | National Youth Strategy: 2022
Framework governing youth policy, youth centers (maisons de jeunes — over 300 nationwide), summer camp programs, and youth civic engagement initiatives. The Ministry of Youth and Sports coordinates implementation. Tunisia has a young population (median age ~31); youth unemployment remains a structural challenge (30%+ rate for 15–24). The 2022 National Youth Strategy targets youth entrepreneurship, digital skills, combating irregular emigration ("harraga"), and volunteer service. The Startup Act (2018) is the principal legislative tool for youth entrepreneurship.
SportSocial

Anti-Doping Regulations — Law No. 2015-50 of December 21, 2015

Enacted: December 21, 2015 | In force: 2016
Establishes Tunisia's national anti-doping legal framework, implementing the World Anti-Doping Code (WADA). Creates the Tunisian Anti-Doping Control Agency (LTDLA — Laboratoire Tunisien de Contrôle du Dopage et des Analyses). Mandates testing programs for national and international competitions, out-of-competition testing, and therapeutic use exemptions (TUEs). Sets sanctions aligned with WADA rules: 2-year ban for first offense, 4 years for aggravated violation. Implements UNESCO International Convention against Doping in Sport (ratified 2006).
SportCriminal

Draft Organic Law on Sports Structures (pending)

Approved in principle: Council of Ministers, November 29, 2025 | Under review: ARP Education, Vocational Training, Scientific Research, Youth & Sports Commission (since March 5, 2026) | Status: not yet enacted as of July 2, 2026
A government-drafted organic law, reported at 211 articles across 7 chapters, that would repeal and replace the Sport Law (Organic Law No. 1995-11 of February 6, 1995, above). Presented to the Council of Ministers on November 29, 2025 by Youth and Sports Minister Sadok Mourali, under Prime Minister Sarra Zaafrani Zenzri, after revision based on inter-ministerial and comparative-law input. Stated objectives: democratizing governance of sports structures, guaranteeing transparency, modernizing sports-club management, and improving financing mechanisms and the sustainability of public sports services. Reported provisions include Art. 13 (limiting federation/club executive-bureau officers to three terms) and Art. 120 (authorizing conversion of sports clubs into professional sports companies). Under examination by the ARP's Education, Vocational Training, Scientific Research, Youth and Sports Commission since a March 5, 2026 session, with expert hearings on sports law and sports arbitration continuing into spring 2026. At a May 15, 2026 hearing, sports-law experts (Fethi Mouldi, Maher Snoussi) publicly warned the draft grants the supervising ministry excessive powers — including appointing members of clubs' ethics/governance boards and an approval right over international representation — that could conflict with international sports-body autonomy requirements (risking non-recognition of Tunisian federations) despite the bill's stated "independence" objective; concerns were also raised about compliance burdens on small amateur clubs. Update (Run 30): following further expert testimony (including the Tunisian Olympic Committee, the Paralympic Committee, and a sports-law association), the commission has moved past the hearing stage into drafting its committee report — a modest procedural step beyond the May 15 hearing checkpoint — though no committee vote or plenary date has been confirmed. Update (Run 343): a July 16, 2026 African Manager report confirms the commission is still in active examination, more than a year after the November 2025 Council of Ministers presentation, and adds two provisions not previously documented here: an anti-cumul-of-office rule restricting individuals from simultaneously holding leadership positions across multiple sports federations or clubs, and a decision to strip the bill of its disciplinary and dispute-resolution provisions entirely, on the rationale that internal discipline and dispute resolution are matters for the federations' own statutes rather than state organic law. No committee vote or plenary date has been confirmed. Not yet enacted as of this run. Update (Run 344): a fuller read of the same July 16, 2026 African Manager coverage clarifies the anti-cumul-of-office rule is not absolute: it carves out an explicit exception permitting an individual to simultaneously hold a sports-federation or club executive-bureau seat alongside a leadership position at the Tunisian Olympic Committee or Tunisian Paralympic Committee, reflecting those two bodies' distinct national-umbrella role rather than ordinary federation/club governance. No committee vote or plenary date confirmed. Update (Run 346): a July 16, 2026 African Manager report on the same commission session confirms the examination has moved on to candidacy-condition provisions for federation/club leadership roles — a "legal capacity" (capacité juridique) requirement, a minimum baccalaureate-level education requirement, a minimum management-experience threshold, and female-representation quota rules — corroborating and dating the single-source, undated Espace Manager lead that Run 345 had deliberately withheld for lack of verification. No committee vote or plenary date confirmed; not yet enacted as of this run. Update (Run 353): a July 16, 2026 committee session (chaired by Abderrazek Aouidet, with vice-president Mounir Kammouni and rapporteur Néjib Akremi) resolved one of the candidacy-condition sub-debates flagged at Run 346: on whether legally-resident foreigners could stand for sports-club and federation directing-committee seats, the majority position limits them to ordinary member status only, excluding them from the president or vice-president roles; the existing bar on holding the presidency of, or belonging to, more than one sports structure simultaneously was reaffirmed, alongside the Olympic/Paralympic Committee cumul exception already documented at Run 344. No committee vote or plenary date confirmed; not yet enacted as of this run.
SportPending

Proposition de loi n° 83/2025 (pending)Combating Violence in and around Stadiums

Filed: July 2025 | Under review: Education Commission (ARP) | Status: not yet enacted as of July 1, 2026
A parliamentary bill creating a dedicated criminal framework for violence at sporting events, filling a gap left by the Sport Law (No. 1995-11) and the general Penal Code. Covers acts of violence in stadiums, sports facilities, and surrounding gathering areas nationwide. Proposes: 6 months' to 5 years' imprisonment for acts of violence at sporting events; a minimum 5,000 TND fine for throwing projectiles or using flares/pyrotechnics; stadium bans of at least 5 years (life for repeat offenders); and 5 to 10 years' imprisonment for carrying or using weapons or dangerous objects at sports events. Would create a national committee to monitor stadium violence, with representatives from the Ministry of Youth and Sports, the Ministry of the Interior, the Tunisian Football Federation, civil-society associations, and supporters' groups. Under review by the ARP's Education Commission as of mid-2026; not yet enacted.
SportCriminalPending

🧭 Areas for Future Expansion

This artifact has been maintained across 560+ research runs since late June 2026, each cross-checking Tunisian legal news, JORT publications, and ARP/CNRD proceedings against the existing content before adding anything, to avoid duplication. The overwhelming majority of enacted Tunisian legislation across the 30 sections above is documented; most runs now function as verification passes on a small "standing watchlist" of not-yet-resolved bills below. Run 560 housekeeping note: this section previously carried a 450+ item, 1.4 MB list mixing genuine pending-bill status with hundreds of near-duplicate "still no update" run-confirmation entries (up to 36 consecutive restatements of the same "Foreign Exchange Code — no floor vote yet" fact) and 374 verbose per-run process narratives (branch resets, stale-fork recoveries) that had come to dominate the file's size without adding legal content — every substantive finding they contained was, by the project's own stated practice, already folded into the relevant law-card entry's own "Update (Run N)" annotations. That list has been consolidated to the deduplicated set of genuinely still-open items below; the full run-by-run history remains permanently available in this repository's git commit log and merged pull requests (one per run), so no information was lost, only compacted. Future runs: append new findings to a card's own entry or to this list; avoid re-adding a "still unconfirmed, Nth consecutive pass" line for an item whose status has not changed since its last note here — a single current-status line per item is sufficient.
  • The new Foreign Exchange Code (Draft Law 115/2025, 91 articles) — committee chair Maher Ktari has called continued Central Bank of Tunisia consultation "essential" before final adoption (per a June 23, 2026 report), with the BCT still not having formally responded as of that date; a July 1, 2026 report on the separate Development Plan debate found deputies still publicly urging "acceleration" of this Code's adoption, so Ktari's pre-recess target should be treated as unconfirmed rather than on track. The related exchange-violations amnesty bill (No. 58/2025 — see standalone entry) remains with the Finance Committee with reporting suggesting the two tracks are being harmonized; watch for whether they merge into a single text or advance separately. (Reconfirmed Run 560, July 27, 2026) Still no floor vote or committee report; status unchanged since the June 23, 2026 data point above.
  • The pending Personal Data Protection & AI organic bill and the Associations Decree reform — both remain under active ARP Rights & Freedoms Commission review with no confirmed outcome to the lapsed June 24, 2026 report deadline; as of a June 4, 2026 update, the commission was still awaiting written ministry opinions on data protection, and on associations was awaiting government feedback with civil-society hearings still unscheduled — status essentially stalled since Run 38. Note: the Data Protection bill's article count is reported inconsistently across primary sources (132 vs. 123 articles) and remains unresolved — do not "correct" one figure to the other without a primary JORT/ARP text. Separately (Run 39 addition), see the civil-society-suspensions entry below for Amnesty International/HRW reporting on ~25 associations administratively suspended since mid-2025 under the still-unreformed 2011 decree.
  • The Draft Organic Law on Audiovisual Communication (99 articles, filed February 25, 2025, to replace HAICA) — no plenary movement confirmed; the May 20, 2026 jurisdictional dispute between the Rights & Freedoms Commission and the General Legislation Committee over which body hears the bill remains the last confirmed status, with no independent confirmation that the announced June 2026 hearings occurred.
  • The Investment Code reform bill — approved in principle by the Restricted Ministerial Council on December 31, 2025; the ARP Strategic Planning Commission (chaired by Saber Jelassi) remained at the article-by-article voting stage as of a May 31, 2026 report, with no committee vote or referral to plenary confirmed since. (Added Run 70) A July 3, 2026 IACE report separately classifies this reform as "validated by the Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP" — possibly describing the government's own track as distinct from the committee-stage MP-initiated proposition n°26/2024 noted in the standalone entry; not yet reconciled.
  • A reported but largely unconfirmed new Code de l'eau (Water Code) replacement — its seven-chapter structure is now sourced to Minister Ezzeddine Ben Cheikh's February 25, 2025 Conseil ministériel restreint presentation (traced Run 51), but no draft has been filed with the ARP and no publicly available draft text exists as of this run; the 1975 Code (Law No. 75-16) remains the operative statute. (Sharpened Run 70) now corroborated across three July 3, 2026 sources as "validated by the Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP" — an executive-stage bottleneck, not a drafting one.
  • (Added Run 70) The Hydrocarbons Code amendment (see standalone Law No. 99-93 entry) — examined by a restricted ministerial council February 7, 2025, renewed as an "urgent" priority by a June 26, 2026 press report amid declining production, and confirmed by the July 3, 2026 IACE report as "validated by the Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP," alongside the Investment, Water, and Environment codes above.
  • The gambling/online-betting reform bills remain under General Legislation Committee review since a January 29, 2026 referral, with no confirmed timeline; the Tax Adviser Organic Law (proposition de loi organique n° 2023-013) showed incremental forward movement — a June 3–4, 2026 session was reported as the commission's "last examination session" on disputed articles — but no confirmed article-by-article vote conclusion or committee-report transmission has followed as of this run, so it remains formally not finalized.
  • The Proposition de loi n° 42/2024 (social-media sales cahier des charges, consumer-protection chapter, 3-day right of withdrawal) already documented under Digital/Commercial Law — a June 24, 2026 secondary source described the working group's text as reaching a "near-final" shape, but no committee vote or report is confirmed, so the cahier-des-charges-vs-electronic-declaration debate remains formally open; beyond it, no comprehensive general e-commerce statute has been identified.
  • CNAM / national health-insurance reform — the January 2026 tripartite payment-guarantee agreement and the July 1, 2026 pharmacist payment-commitment addressed successive liquidity flashpoints (see standalone entry) amid a ~TND 1.95 billion 2025 debt to public hospitals, but a structural reform bill (splitting social-service management from health-insurance management) has still not reached the ARP. (Superseded Run 309 — corrects a stale bullet left unsynced with the standalone entry's Run 255 update since Run 46) The private biologists' union's planned July 13, 2026 tiers-payant suspension was itself called off on July 10, 2026 after the union met the Minister of Social Affairs and CNAM, which committed to settling its outstanding debt to private laboratories within an agreed timeframe; billing has continued uninterrupted at private labs since, as with the July 1 pharmacist fix, a negotiated stopgap rather than the underlying structural-financing reform.
  • The Combating Financial Exclusion bill (Draft Law No. 023/2024, its number corrected in Run 43 — see standalone entry) and the related but separate "banque postale" bill (Proposition de loi n° 82/2025, now its own standalone entry) both remain temporized/under review as of June 28, 2026, confirmed unchanged; the Proposition de loi n° 83/2025 (stadium violence) and the nationality-code revision bill (n° 17/2026, 36 co-sponsors, filed by Deputy Syrine Mrabet, examined by committee May 7 and June 4, 2026) both remain under committee review with no vote scheduled and no progress reported since Run 38.
  • (Updated Run 311) The National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad reform bill (n° 2024/064 — see Nationality section) — the ARP Foreign Affairs Committee moved from hearings to adopting amendments on July 14, 2026 (adding permanent diaspora-constituency deputy members plus 60 diaspora representatives to the Council's general assembly), still short of a completed committee report or plenary vote; and the cinema-industry bill (Proposition de loi n° 2024-03 — see Tourism/Culture section) — a June 24, 2026 session was described as "continuing the study" rather than a completed article-by-article finalization (corrected in Run 39 from an earlier overstatement), and still awaits its committee report. Both should be watched for referral to plenary.
